<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that ellipsoids uniquely minimize the volume of the projection body among convex bodies of fixed volume in every dimension at least four. This proves Petty's projection-volume conjecture in these dimensions.

- **thm-main** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every integer $n\ge4$ and every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{eq:main} \frac{\|\Pi K\|}{\|K\|^{n-1}} \ge \kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K=a+TB_2^n$ for some $a\in\mathbb R^n$ and some invertible linear map $T$.
  - Explanation: Ellipsoids are precisely the global minimizers of normalized projection volume.
- **lem-gauge-variation** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-gauge-variation)): Let $s:S^{n-1}\to(0,\infty)$ and $\phi:S^{n-1}\to\mathbb R$ be continuous. For sufficiently small $\|t\|$, define $$W_t=\bigcap_{v\in S^{n-1}}\{x\in\mathbb R^n:x\cdot v\le s(v)+t\phi(v)\}, \qquad g_t=g_{W_t}.$$ Then $W_t$ is a convex body containing the origin in its interior, and $$\label{eq:gauge-formulas} g_t(x)=\max_{v\in S^{n-1}}\frac{x\cdot v}{s(v)+t\phi(v)}, \qquad \frac{\|W_t\|}{\kappa_n}=\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_t(u)^{-n}.$$ For almost every $u\in S^{n-1}$, the maximizing vector $v/s(v)$ at $t=0$ is unique and equals the Euclidean gradient $\nabla g_0(u)$. At these directions, $$\label{eq:gauge-derivative} \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\|_{t=0}g_t(u) =-g_0(u)\phi\bigl(\nabla g_0(u)\bigr).$$ On the sphere the gauges are uniformly bounded above and away from zero for small $\|t\|$, and the difference quotients $(g_t-g_0)/t$ are uniformly bounded. In particular, $$\label{eq:constraint-volume-derivative} \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\|_{t=0}\|W_t\| =n\kappa_n\mathbb E_{\sigma}\!\left[ g_0(u)^{-n}\phi\bigl(\nabla g_0(u)\bigr)\right].$$ For $s=h_L$, one has $W_0=L$.
  - Explanation: Differentiates gauges and volumes under continuous supporting-constraint variations.
- **thm-brunn-minkowski** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-brunn-minkowski)): For every integer $d\ge1$ and convex bodies $A,B\subset\mathbb R^d$, write $\|\cdot\|$ for $d$-dimensional volume. For $0\le t\le1$, $$\label{eq:brunn-minkowski} \|(1-t)A+tB\|^{1/d} \ge (1-t)\|A\|^{1/d}+t\|B\|^{1/d}.$$ If $0<t<1$, equality holds if and only if $B=a+cA$ for some $a\in\mathbb R^d$ and $c>0$.
  - Explanation: Gives the volume inequality and its homothety equality condition.
- **cor-volume-first-variation** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-volume-first-variation)): Let $K,M\subset\mathbb R^n$ be convex bodies, with the origin interior to $K$. Then $$\begin{aligned} \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right\|_{0+}\|K+tM\| &=n\kappa_n\mathbb E_{\sigma}\!\left[g_K(u)^{-n} h_M\bigl(\nabla g_K(u)\bigr)\right] \label{eq:minkowski-volume-derivative}\\ &\ge n\|K\|^{(n-1)/n}\|M\|^{1/n}.\nonumber \end{aligned}$$ Equality in the inequality holds if and only if $K$ and $M$ are homothetic up to translation. The derivative identity remains valid when $M$ is an arbitrary nonempty compact convex set.
  - Explanation: Expresses volume first variation and identifies its sharp homothety case.
- **prop-projection-measure** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-projection-measure)): Translate and dilate a convex body $K$ so that $0\in\operatorname{int}K$ and $\|K\|=\kappa_n$. Define $\eta_K$ by $$\label{eq:eta-definition} \int \psi(x)\,d\eta_K(x) =\mathbb E_{\sigma}\!\left[g_K(u)^{-n}\psi\bigl(\nabla g_K(u)\bigr)\right]$$ for nonnegative Borel functions $\psi$; the choice of the gradient on its null exceptional set is immaterial. Then $\eta_K$ is an admissible probability measure of bounded support. Equality in <a href="#eq:functional-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-condition">[eq:functional-condition]</a> for $\eta_K$ and a symmetric body $M$ holds if and only if $K$ is a translate of a positive dilate of $M$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:projection-measure} h_{\Pi K}(y)=\frac{n\kappa_n}{2} \int \|x\cdot y\|\,d\eta_K(x) \qquad(y\in\mathbb R^n),$$ and $$\label{eq:eta-normalization} \int h_K(x)\,d\eta_K(x)=1.$$ For the unit ball, $\eta_{B_2^n}=\sigma$, and consequently $$\label{eq:ball-constant} \frac{n\kappa_n b_n}{2}=\kappa_{n-1}.$$
  - Explanation: Associates an admissible measure to a normalized convex body and represents its projection body.
- **lem-spherical-harmonics** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-spherical-harmonics)): The spaces $\mathcal H_l$ form an orthogonal decomposition of $L^2(\sigma)$, and $\Delta_SY=-\lambda_lY$ for $Y\in\mathcal H_l$. Every even $H\in C^1(S^{n-1})$ satisfies $$\label{eq:spectral-gap} \lambda_2\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(H)+(\lambda_4-\lambda_2)\left\lVert QH\right\rVert_2^2 \le \mathbb E_{\sigma}\left\|\nabla_SH\right\|^2, \qquad \lambda_2=2n,\quad \lambda_4=4(n+2).$$
  - Explanation: Separates variance from higher even spherical harmonics through the spherical gradient.
- **lem-sign-operator** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-sign-operator)): For even $C^1$ functions, $$\label{eq:sign-operator} \mathcal B(f_1,f_2) =\left\langle f_1, \left[C+\left(\frac{2p}{k}+\frac{p^2}{k^2}\right)(C-P)\right] f_2\right\rangle.$$
  - Explanation: Represents the spherical sign form using the cosine and Funk transforms.
- **lem-spherical-multipliers** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-spherical-multipliers)): On $\mathcal H_{2j}$ the operators $P$ and $C$ are scalar multipliers, with $$\label{eq:transform-multipliers} P_{2j}=(-1)^j \frac{1\cdot3\cdots(2j-1)}{p(p+2)\cdots(p+2j-2)}, \qquad C_{2j}=\frac{pP_{2j}}{p-\lambda_{2j}},$$ where empty products equal one. In particular, $$\label{eq:cosine-recurrence} \begin{gathered} C_0=1,\qquad C_2=\frac1{p+2},\qquad C_4=-\frac1{(p+2)(p+4)},\\[3pt] \frac{C_{l+2}}{C_l}=-\frac{l-1}{l+p+2}\quad(l\ge2\text{ even}). \end{gathered}$$
  - Explanation: Computes the transform multipliers and their recurrence.
- **prop-sign-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-sign-estimate)): Let $k>0$ and let $f_1,f_2$ be even $C^1$ functions on $S^{n-1}$. If $f_1$ has zero projection onto $\mathcal H_2$, then $$\label{eq:sign-bound} \mathcal B(f_1,f_2) \ge (\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_1)(\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_2) -c_{n,k}\left\lVert Qf_1\right\rVert_2\left\lVert Qf_2\right\rVert_2.$$ The operator identity <a href="#eq:sign-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-operator">[eq:sign-operator]</a> and this estimate also hold when $f_i=g_i^k$ for arbitrary norms $g_i$ on $\mathbb R^n$, with the vector fields defined almost everywhere. In that case $$\label{eq:norm-vector-field} X_{g_i^k}(u)=g_i(u)^{k-1}\nabla g_i(u).$$
  - Explanation: Controls the higher harmonic contribution when the degree-two component vanishes.
- **thm-norm-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-norm-estimate)): Let $g$ be a norm on $\mathbb R^n$, where $n\ge4$, normalized by $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{-n}=1$ on $S^{n-1}$. Then $$\label{eq:norm-estimate} c_{n,k}\\|Q(g^k)\\|_2^2 \le (\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^k)^2-(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1})^2.$$ The inequality is strict unless $g=1$ on $S^{n-1}$.
  - Explanation: Bounds higher harmonics of normalized norm powers, strictly away from the Euclidean norm.
- **lem-curvature** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-curvature)): For every norm $g$ on $\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{eq:norm-curvature} 2n\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G(g))+d_*\\|QG(g)\\|_2^2 \le p\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[gI(g)],\qquad p=n-1.$$
  - Explanation: Uses convexity to control variance and higher harmonics of the scalar transformation.
- **lem-scalar-estimates** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-estimates)): The constants and functions above satisfy $$\label{eq:scalar-mean-bound} c\left(\frac{2n\beta^2}{wd_*} -\frac{(1-\beta)^2}{1-w}\right) \le\frac{2k-1}{k^2}$$ and $$\label{eq:scalar-pointwise} \mathcal L(z)\le2W(z)\qquad(z>0).$$ Equality in <a href="#eq:scalar-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-pointwise">[eq:scalar-pointwise]</a> holds only at $z=1$.
  - Explanation: Supplies the scalar inequalities needed for the strict norm estimate.
- **lem-minimizer** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-minimizer)): For every nonzero symmetric positive semidefinite $A$, there is a unique origin-symmetric convex body $L_A$ in $V=\mathop{\mathrm{range}}(A)$ that minimizes <a href="#eq:variational-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variational-objective">[eq:variational-objective]</a> subject to $\tau_A(L_A)=1$. Denote its extended gauge by $g_A$.
  - Explanation: Establishes existence and uniqueness of the normalized variational minimizing body.
- **prop-representation** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-representation)): Suppose $A$ is positive definite, and let $L$ be any positive dilate of $L_A$, with gauge $g=g_L$. For every continuous even function $\phi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ that is positively homogeneous of degree one, $$\label{eq:representation} \int\phi(Ax)\,d\eta(x) =s\,\mathbb E_{\sigma}\phi\bigl(X_{g^k}(u)\bigr), \qquad s=\frac{\tau_A(L)}{\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1}}.$$ Here, almost everywhere on the sphere, $$\label{eq:norm-power-field} X_{g^k}=g^ku+g^{k-1}\nabla_Sg=g^{k-1}\nabla g.$$
  - Explanation: Represents homogeneous support tests by the minimizing norm vector field.
- **lem-matrix-continuity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-matrix-continuity)): The map $A\mapsto g_A\|_{S^{n-1}}$ is continuous on $\mathcal A$ in the uniform norm.
  - Explanation: Shows uniform continuity of minimizing gauges through matrix rank changes.
- **lem-fixed-point** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-fixed-point)): Every continuous self-map of a nonempty compact convex subset of a finite-dimensional Euclidean space has a fixed point.
  - Explanation: States the compact convex fixed-point principle used for affine positioning.
- **prop-position** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-position)): There is a symmetric positive definite matrix $A$ with $\det A=1$ such that $g_A^k$ has zero component in $\mathcal H_2$.
  - Explanation: Selects an invertible affine position eliminating the degree-two harmonic component.
- **thm-bilinear** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-bilinear)): Let $\eta,\zeta$ be finite positive measures on $\mathbb R^n$ with bounded, spanning support. Suppose that both satisfy <a href="#eq:functional-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-condition">[eq:functional-condition]</a>. Then $$\label{eq:bilinear} \iint \|x\cdot y\|\,d\eta(x)d\zeta(y)\ge b_n.$$ If equality holds, there is an origin-centered ellipsoid $E_0$ such that $$\int h_{E_0}\,d\eta=(\|E_0\|/\kappa_n)^{1/n}.$$
  - Explanation: Bounds the pairing of admissible measures and extracts an ellipsoid when equality holds.
- **cor-lutwak-petty** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-lutwak-petty)): Let $n\ge3$ and $1\le i\le n-1$ be integers, and let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex body with nonempty interior. Then $$\label{eq:lutwak-petty} \begin{aligned} \frac{V_{i+1}(\Pi_iK)}{V_{i+1}(K)^i} &\ge \frac{V_{i+1}(\Pi_iB_2^n)}{V_{i+1}(B_2^n)^i}\\ &= \frac{\left(\displaystyle\binom{n-1}{i} \frac{\kappa_{n-1}}{\kappa_{n-i-1}}\right)^{i+1}} {\left(\displaystyle\binom{n}{i+1} \frac{\kappa_n}{\kappa_{n-i-1}}\right)^{i-1}}. \end{aligned}$$ The lower bound is sharp, since $K=B_2^n$ attains it.
  - Explanation: Gives sharp Euclidean-ball lower bounds for lower-degree projection-body ratios.
- **cor-affine-sobolev** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-affine-sobolev)): Let $n\ge3$ be an integer, put $q=n/(n-1)$, and let $f_1,\ldots,f_n\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n)$ be nonnegative. Then $$\label{eq:mixed-affine-sobolev} \mathcal D_n(f_1,\ldots,f_n) \ge n!\,\kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n} \prod_{i=1}^n\left\lVert f_i\right\rVert_q.$$ In particular, every nonnegative $f\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n)$ satisfies the affine $L^1$ Sobolev inequality $$\label{eq:affine-sobolev} \mathcal D_n(f,\ldots,f)^{1/n} \ge (n!)^{1/n}\kappa_{n-1}\kappa_n^{(2-n)/n}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_q.$$ Both constants are sharp in this class, through smooth approximation of ellipsoid indicators in $BV(\mathbb R^n)$; no equality assertion for nonzero $C_c^1$ functions is made.
  - Explanation: Gives sharp determinant-gradient and affine Sobolev bounds through smooth approximation.
- **cor-holmes-thompson** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-holmes-thompson)): Let $n\ge3$, let $B=-B\subset\mathbb R^n$ be the unit ball of a real normed space, and let $C\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex body. Write $B^\circ$ for the polar unit ball and put $I_B=\Pi(B^\circ)/\kappa_{n-1}$. Normalize the Holmes–Thompson volume and boundary area by $$\mathcal V_B(C)=\frac{\|B^\circ\|}{\kappa_n}\|C\|, \qquad \mathcal A_B(\partial C)=nV(C[n-1],I_B),$$ where $V(C[n-1],I_B)$ is mixed volume with $n-1$ copies of $C$, normalized by $V(C[n])=\|C\|$. Then $$\label{eq:holmes-thompson} \mathcal A_B(\partial C)^n \ge n^n\kappa_n\,\mathcal V_B(C)^{n-1}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $B$ is an ellipsoid and $C=x+tB$ for some $x\in\mathbb R^n$ and $t>0$.
  - Explanation: Gives the normed-space isoperimetric inequality and its ellipsoidal equality condition.

        "exact_quote": "Translate and dilate a convex body $K$ so that $0\\in\\operatorname{int}K$ and $|K|=\\kappa_n$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-spherical-multipliers",
        "exact_quote": "On $\\mathcal H_{2j}$ the operators $P$ and $C$ are scalar multipliers, with $$\\label{eq:transform-multipliers}\n P_{2j}=(-1)^j\n \\frac{1\\cdot3\\cdots(2j-1)}{p(p+2)\\cdots(p+2j-2)},\n \\qquad\n C_{2j}=\\frac{pP_{2j}}{p-\\lambda_{2j}},$$ where empty products equal one.         "source_id": "PM-088.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-bilinear",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\eta,\\zeta$ be finite positive measures on $\\mathbb R^n$ with bounded, spanning support. Suppose that both satisfy <a href=\"#eq:functional-condition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:functional-condition\">[eq:functional-condition]</a>. Then $$\\label{eq:bilinear}\n \\iint |x\\cdot y|\\,d\\eta(x)d\\zeta(y)\\ge b_n.$$ If equality holds, there is an origin-centered ellipsoid $E_0$ such that $$\\int h_{E_0}\\,d\\eta=(|E_0|/\\kappa_n)^{1/n}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-088.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

          "exact_quote": "Let $\\eta,\\zeta$ be finite positive measures on $\\mathbb R^n$ with bounded, spanning support. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Projection body** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, its projection body $\Pi K$ is the origin-symmetric convex body whose support function is $$h_{\Pi K}(u)=\operatorname{vol}_{n-1} \bigl(\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}K\bigr), \qquad u\in S^{n-1}.$$
- **Support function** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Here a convex body is compact, convex, and has nonempty interior, and $h_L(x)=\max_{y\in L}\left\langle x,y\right\rangle$ denotes its support function.
- **Normalized projection volume** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The normalized projection volume is $$R_n(K)=\frac{\|\Pi K\|}{\|K\|^{n-1}}.$$
- **Gauge** ([source](#pm-h-7)): For a convex body $L$ containing the origin in its interior, its *gauge* is $$g_L(x)=\inf\{a>0:x\in aL\}.$$
- **Support functional** ([source](#pm-h-9)): For a finite positive Borel measure $\eta$ on $\mathbb R^n$ with bounded support, write $$\tau_\eta(M)=\int h_M(x)\,d\eta(x).$$
- **Spherical harmonics** ([source](#pm-h-11)): Let $\mathcal H_l$ be the restrictions to $S^{n-1}$ of homogeneous harmonic polynomials of degree $l$, and put $$\lambda_l=l(l+n-2)=l(l+p-1).$$
- **Higher-harmonic projection** ([source](#pm-h-11)): For an even function, let $Q$ denote orthogonal projection onto the closed sum of $\mathcal H_4,\mathcal H_6,\ldots$.
- **Sign-test vector field** ([source](#eq:vector-field)): For $k>0$ and an even differentiable function $f$ on $S^{n-1}$, define $$\label{eq:vector-field} X_f(u)=f(u)u+k^{-1}\nabla_Sf(u).$$
- **Admissible measure** ([source](#stmt-def-admissible-measure)): We call $\eta$ *admissible* if its support spans $\mathbb R^n$ and $$\label{eq:functional-condition} \tau_\eta(M)\ge\left(\frac{\|M\|}{\kappa_n}\right)^{1/n} \quad\text{for every origin-symmetric convex body }M\subset\mathbb R^n.$$ The measure itself is not required to be symmetric.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| lambda-two | \lambda_2 | = | $2n$ | Degree-two spherical harmonics. |
| lambda-four | \lambda_4 | = | $4(n+2)$ | Degree-four spherical harmonics. |
| cosine-zero | C_0 | = | $1$ | Constant spherical harmonics. |
| cosine-two | C_2 | = | $\frac1{p+2}$ | Degree-two spherical harmonics. |
| cosine-four | C_4 | = | $-\frac1{(p+2)(p+4)}$ | Degree-four spherical harmonics. |
| spectral-difference | d_* | = | $2n+8$ | The difference of the degree-four and degree-two eigenvalues. |
| tail-four | c | = | $\frac{121}{35}$ | Dimension four with the stated first-power choice. |
| tail-higher | c | = | $\frac{n^2+5n+7}{(n+1)(n+3)}$ | Dimensions at least five with the stated square-power choice. |
| large-leading | A_4 | = | $\frac{2n-8}{3}$ | Dimensions at least seven in the scalar polynomial expansion. |
| large-quadratic | B_2 | = | $16+\frac{64}{n}$ | Dimensions at least seven in the scalar polynomial expansion. |
| shift-V | V_2(7+t) | = | $5t^3+113t^2+806t+1764$ | Nonnegative shift parameter. |
| shift-R | R(7+t) | = | $7t^4+204t^3+2039t^2+7414t+4256$ | Nonnegative shift parameter. |
| projection-minimum | R_n(K) | ≥ | $\kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n}$ | Every convex body in the stated dimensions at least four. |
| measure-pairing | \iint \|x\cdot y\|\,d\eta(x)d\zeta(y) | ≥ | $b_n$ | Both measures satisfy the support-functional condition; the standing dimension assumption applies. |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **measure** [claim](#stmt-prop-projection-measure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-projection-measure): Gauge first variation and Brunn–Minkowski equality. -> Admissible projection measure.
- **spectrum** [claim](#stmt-prop-sign-estimate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-sign-estimate): Compute cosine and Funk spectra, then estimate the higher harmonic tail. -> Bilinear sign estimate.
- **norm** [claim](#stmt-thm-norm-estimate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-estimates): Use convexity, spectral separation and strict scalar polynomial bounds. -> Strict norm-power estimate.
- **representation** [claim](#stmt-prop-representation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-representation): Differentiate both signs of a Wulff variation at a variational minimum. -> Homogeneous-test representation.
- **continuity** [claim](#stmt-lem-matrix-continuity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-matrix-continuity): Compactness, uniform integrability and uniqueness identify limits across rank changes. -> Continuous minimizer gauges.
- **position** [claim](#stmt-prop-position) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-position): Use an inward matrix field and compact convex fixed-point principle. -> Vanishing degree-two component.
- **pairing** [claim](#stmt-thm-bilinear) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-bilinear): Apply contragredient coordinates and absorb the harmonic error. -> Admissible-measure pairing lower bound.
- **main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-bilinear-2): Apply the pairing to the body and its normalized projection body, retaining sharpness. -> Global projection-volume inequality and ellipsoid equality.

<!-- PM:BEGIN pm.roadmap.v2 -->

    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-prop-projection-measure",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-projection-measure",
          "exact_quote": "Translate and dilate a convex body $K$ so that $0\\in\\operatorname{int}K$ and $|K|=\\kappa_n$. Define $\\eta_K$ by $$\\label{eq:eta-definition}\n \\int \\psi(x)\\,d\\eta_K(x)\n =\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}\\!\\left[g_K(u)^{-n}\\psi\\bigl(\\nabla g_K(u)\\bigr)\\right]$$ for nonnegative Borel functions $\\psi$; the choice of the gradient on its null exceptional set is immaterial. Then $\\eta_K$ is an admissible probability measure of bounded support. Equality in <a href=\"#eq:functional-condition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:functional-condition\">[eq:functional-condition]</a> for $\\eta_K$ and a symmetric body $M$ holds if and only if $K$ is a translate of a positive dilate of $M$. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:projection-measure}\n h_{\\Pi K}(y)=\\frac{n\\kappa_n}{2}\n                  \\int |x\\cdot y|\\,d\\eta_K(x)\n \\qquad(y\\in\\mathbb R^n),$$ and $$\\label{eq:eta-normalization}\n \\int h_K(x)\\,d\\eta_K(x)=1.$$ For the unit ball, $\\eta_{B_2^n}=\\sigma$, and consequently $$\\label{eq:ball-constant}\n \\frac{n\\kappa_n b_n}{2}=\\kappa_{n-1}.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-088.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "measure",
      "inputs": [
        "Convex bodies and support-function measures."
      ],
      "method": "Gauge first variation and Brunn–Minkowski equality.",
      "output": "Admissible projection measure.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-projection-measure"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-prop-sign-estimate",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-sign-estimate",
          "exact_quote": "Let $k>0$ and let $f_1,f_2$ be even $C^1$ functions on $S^{n-1}$. If $f_1$ has zero projection onto $\\mathcal H_2$, then $$\\label{eq:sign-bound}\n \\mathcal B(f_1,f_2)\n \\ge (\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}f_1)(\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}f_2)\n       -c_{n,k}\\left\\lVert Qf_1\\right\\rVert_2\\left\\lVert Qf_2\\right\\rVert_2.$$ The operator identity <a href=\"#eq:sign-operator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:sign-operator\">[eq:sign-operator]</a> and this estimate also hold when $f_i=g_i^k$ for arbitrary norms $g_i$ on $\\mathbb R^n$, with the vector fields defined almost everywhere. In that case $$\\label{eq:norm-vector-field}\n X_{g_i^k}(u)=g_i(u)^{k-1}\\nabla g_i(u).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-088.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "spectrum",
      "inputs": [
        "Convex bodies and support-function measures."
      ],
      "method": "Compute cosine and Funk spectra, then estimate the higher harmonic tail.",
      "output": "Bilinear sign estimate.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-sign-estimate"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-thm-norm-estimate",
      "depends_on": [
        "spectrum"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-norm-estimate",
          "exact_quote": "Let $g$ be a norm on $\\mathbb R^n$, where $n\\ge4$, normalized by $\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}g^{-n}=1$ on $S^{n-1}$. Then $$\\label{eq:norm-estimate}\n c_{n,k}\\|Q(g^k)\\|_2^2\n \\le (\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}g^k)^2-(\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}g^{k-1})^2.$$ The inequality is strict unless $g=1$ on $S^{n-1}$.",
          "source_id": "PM-088.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "norm",
      "inputs": [
        "Convex bodies and support-function measures."
      ],
      "method": "Use convexity, spectral separation and strict scalar polynomial bounds.",

    },

      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-prop-representation",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose $A$ is positive definite, and let $L$ be any positive dilate of $L_A$, with gauge $g=g_L$. For every continuous even function $\\phi:\\mathbb R^n\\to\\mathbb R$ that is positively homogeneous of degree one, $$\\label{eq:representation}\n \\int\\phi(Ax)\\,d\\eta(x)\n =s\\,\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}\\phi\\bigl(X_{g^k}(u)\\bigr),\n \\qquad\n s=\\frac{\\tau_A(L)}{\\mathbb E_{\\sigma}g^{k-1}}.$$ Here, almost everywhere on the sphere, $$\\label{eq:norm-power-field}\n X_{g^k}=g^ku+g^{k-1}\\nabla_Sg=g^{k-1}\\nabla g.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-088.1"
        }

Suppose that both satisfy <a href=\"#eq:functional-condition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:functional-condition\">[eq:functional-condition]</a>. Then $$\\label{eq:bilinear}\n \\iint |x\\cdot y|\\,d\\eta(x)d\\zeta(y)\\ge b_n.$$ If equality holds, there is an origin-centered ellipsoid $E_0$ such that $$\\int h_{E_0}\\,d\\eta=(|E_0|/\\kappa_n)^{1/n}.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-088.1"

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-affine**: The normalized projection volume is invariant under translations and invertible linear maps.
- **I-polynomial**: Clearing the negative powers in the scalar gap gives the displayed polynomials in dimensions $4,5,6$.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-equality**: Classify all minimizers of the lower-degree projection-body ratios. -> Sharpness at the ball does not supply an equality classification.
- **A-smooth**: Treat an ellipsoid indicator as a nonzero smooth extremizer for the affine Sobolev inequality. -> Sharpness is obtained through approximation rather than a claimed smooth equality case.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The scalar proof explicitly constructs polynomial certificates in dimensions $4,5,6$. Only those finite coefficient identities are checked; the continuous convex-body inequality and the affine-position existence argument remain outside this check.

```python
# INPUTS: One integer dimension on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: Dimension is in {4,5,6}; use the z>1 scalar branch.
# OUTPUTS: JSON array of all coefficients of P_n(1+x), in ascending degree.
# TIME: Bounded exact symbolic polynomial arithmetic for the admitted dimensions.
# SPACE: Bounded symbolic expressions and coefficient arrays.
# ARITHMETIC: Exact SymPy rational arithmetic; no floating point.
# FAILURE: Reject dimensions outside the admitted finite set.
import sys, json
from sympy import symbols, Rational, cancel, Poly
n=int(sys.stdin.read())
if n not in (4,5,6):
    raise ValueError('dimension outside finite contract')
z,x=symbols('z x')
k=1 if n==4 else 2
beta=Rational(1,2) if n==4 else Rational(2,3)
w=Rational(3,14) if n==4 else Rational(1,3)
p=n-1
d=2*n+8
c=(1+(Rational(2,k)+Rational(p,k*k))*4*(n+2))/((n+1)*(n+3))
e=(z**(-n)-1)/n
M=z**k-1+k*e
W=z**k-z**(k-1)+e
G=beta*M
D=z**k-1-G
I=k*k*((z**(2*k-1)-1)/(2*k-1)-2*(1-z**(k-1-n))/(n+1-k)+(1-z**(-2*n-1))/(2*n+1))
L=c*((p*beta**2*z*I-2*n*G**2)/(w*d)+D**2/(1-w))
scale={4:2880,5:712800,6:221130}[n]
polynomial=Poly(cancel(scale*z**(2*n)*(2*W-L)).subs(z,1+x).expand(),x)
print(json.dumps([str(polynomial.nth(j)) for j in range(polynomial.degree()+1)]))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **dimension-scope** (hypothesis): The main projection-volume theorem treats dimensions at least four. Its extension to dimension three uses a separately proved theorem. Blocked use: ['Applying the internal proof in dimension three without the external result.']
- **convex-body-scope** (hypothesis): The projection-volume result requires compact convex bodies with nonempty interior. Blocked use: ['Applying the stated ratio to nonconvex sets or bodies with vanishing ambient volume.']
- **admissibility-scope** (hypothesis): The bilinear lower bound requires positive measures with bounded, spanning support satisfying the support-functional inequality for every origin-symmetric convex body. Blocked use: ['Using arbitrary sampled directional measures without proving the support-functional condition.']
- **harmonic-position** (hypothesis): The sign estimate requires the degree-two component of the first function to vanish; the affine position is needed for only one measure. Blocked use: ['Dropping the harmonic cancellation requirement or requiring a simultaneous affine position for both measures.']
- **affine-existence** (nonconstructive): The affine position is obtained from continuous minimizers and a fixed-point argument. A finite solver with a termination or error guarantee is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Treating affine-position existence as an implemented numerical procedure.']
- **singular-boundary** (edge_case): Gauges at singular matrix boundary points become seminorms; the selected affine position must be positive definite. Blocked use: ['Substituting a singular boundary seminorm for the final norm or using an inverse of a singular matrix.']
- **lower-degree-equality** (unknown): The lower-degree projection inequalities assert sharpness at the ball without classifying all equality cases. Blocked use: ['Extending the ellipsoid equality classification to every lower-degree ratio.']
- **sobolev-attainment** (approximation): Sharpness for the compactly supported differentiable function class is established by smooth approximation of ellipsoid indicators, without asserting nonzero equality cases in that class. Blocked use: ['Claiming that a nonzero smooth compactly supported function attains the sharp constant.']
- **functional-endpoint** (hypothesis): The functional application is confined to the stated endpoint and nonnegative compactly supported differentiable functions. Blocked use: ['Extending the application to other exponents or sign-changing functions without a separate proof.']
- **holmes-thompson-equality** (edge_case): The Holmes–Thompson equality case requires an ellipsoidal norm ball and a body homothetic to it. Blocked use: ['Claiming equality for every fixed norm ball solely by choosing its isoperimetrix.']
- **finite-certificate-scope** (formal_scope): Exact verification of scalar polynomial identities supports those algebraic steps; a certificate for the continuous geometric theorem is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Presenting bounded arithmetic checks as verification of the full projection-volume theorem.']
- **polar-functional** (counterexample_scope): The polar projection-body inequality concerns a different functional from the unpolarized volume ratio. Blocked use: ['Replacing the projection body by its polar while retaining the same lower bound.']

Scope note of family 088 (`lean/docs/088.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Petty’s projection-volume conjecture in dimensions at least four](../../preprints/Pettys-projection-volume-conjecture-in-dimensions-at-least-four-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A product counterexample to the simplex maximum for projection-body volume](../../preprints/A-product-counterexample-to-the-simplex-maximum-for-projection-body-volume-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Petty's projection-volume conjecture predicts
>
> $\displaystyle \frac{|\Pi K|}{|K|^{n-1}}\ge \kappa_{n-1}^{n}\kappa_n^{2-n},$
>
> with equality exactly for ellipsoids; here $\kappa_j$ is the volume of the Euclidean unit ball in dimension $j$. The formalization establishes this for every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ and every $n\ge4$.
>
> Other inequalities in the projection-body family are not included.
>
> Brannen's simplex-maximization conjecture predicts that a simplex maximizes normalized projection-body volume $|\Pi K|/|K|^{n-1}$ in dimension $n$. The formalized counterexample is the product of two ten-dimensional simplices. Its normalized projection volume, divided by that of a twenty-dimensional simplex, is
>
> $\displaystyle \frac{22{,}355{,}476}{22{,}020{,}096}>1.$
>
> Thus the proposed maximum fails in dimension $20$. The paper's exponential-factor result for every sufficiently large dimension is not included.

Comparator source `PettyProjectionVolume.lean` (1769 bytes, sha256 `29270000c1c8332fef7657ed9f81f89a472d902e2a8a6eb4c4a28934c9f528f3`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open Set MeasureTheory
open scoped RealInnerProductSpace

namespace PettyProjection

abbrev Space (n : ℕ) := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)

def IsConvexBody {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Space n)) : Prop :=
  IsCompact K ∧ Convex ℝ K ∧ (interior K).Nonempty

def unitBall (n : ℕ) : Set (Space n) := Metric.closedBall 0 1

def kappa (n : ℕ) : ℝ := volume.real (unitBall n)

def perpendicular {n : ℕ} (u : Space n) : Submodule ℝ (Space n) :=
  (Submodule.span ℝ {u})ᗮ

/-- Volume in the induced Euclidean structure on the orthogonal hyperplane. -/
def shadowVolume {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Space n)) (u : Space n) : ℝ :=
  (volume : Measure (perpendicular u)).real
    ((perpendicular u).orthogonalProjectionOnto '' K)

/-- The body with support function equal to the volumes of orthogonal shadows,
expressed as its defining intersection of supporting half-spaces. -/
def projectionBody {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Space n)) : Set (Space n) :=
  {x | ∀ u : Space n, ‖u‖ = 1 → ⟪u, x⟫ ≤ shadowVolume K u}

def projectionRatio {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Space n)) : ℝ :=
  volume.real (projectionBody K) / volume.real K ^ (n - 1)

def pettyConstant (n : ℕ) : ℝ :=
  kappa (n - 1) ^ n * kappa n ^ (2 - (n : ℤ))

def IsEllipsoid {n : ℕ} (K : Set (Space n)) : Prop :=
  ∃ (a : Space n) (T : Space n ≃ₗ[ℝ] Space n),
    K = (fun x => a + T x) '' unitBall n

/-- The exact unrestricted inequality and its global equality characterization.
-/
theorem petty_projection_volume (n : ℕ) (hn : 4 ≤ n)
    (K : Set (Space n)) (hK : IsConvexBody K) :
    pettyConstant n ≤ projectionRatio K ∧
      (projectionRatio K = pettyConstant n ↔ IsEllipsoid K) := by
  sorry

end PettyProjection
end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that ellipsoids uniquely minimize the volume of the projection body among convex bodies of fixed volume in every dimension at least four. This proves Petty's projection-volume conjecture in these dimensions.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, its projection body $\Pi K$ is the origin-symmetric convex body whose support function is $$h_{\Pi K}(u)=\operatorname{vol}_{n-1}
       \bigl(\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}K\bigr),
       \qquad u\in S^{n-1}.$$ Here a convex body is compact, convex, and has nonempty interior, and $h_L(x)=\max_{y\in L}\left\langle x,y\right\rangle$ denotes its support function. Thus $\Pi K$ records the volumes of the orthogonal shadows of $K$ in all directions. Write $|L|=\operatorname{vol}_n(L)$ and let $B_2^m$ be the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb R^m$, with volume $\kappa_m$. The normalized projection volume is $$R_n(K)=\frac{|\Pi K|}{|K|^{n-1}}.$$ It is invariant under translations and invertible linear maps: the classical contravariance identity is $\Pi(TK)=|\det T|T^{-t}\Pi K$; see <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Ludwig2002](#ref-Ludwig2002), Equation (1)]. We verify this identity directly in Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a>.

Petty’s projection-volume conjecture asserts, for $n\ge3$, that $R_n$ is minimized precisely by ellipsoids <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Petty1971](#ref-Petty1971)]. The ball satisfies $\Pi B_2^n=\kappa_{n-1}B_2^n$, so the proposed minimum is fixed by the Euclidean case. This is an affine isoperimetric problem in which directional projection data are assembled into a second body before its volume is measured. The classical Petty inequality for the *polar* of the projection body concerns a different functional; see <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Gardner2002](#ref-Gardner2002), Section 18.6]. We prove the unpolarized conjecture in every dimension at least four.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *For every integer $n\ge4$ and every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{eq:main}
 \frac{|\Pi K|}{|K|^{n-1}}
 \ge \kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $K=a+TB_2^n$ for some $a\in\mathbb R^n$ and some invertible linear map $T$.*

</div>

The theorem includes bodies without symmetry or boundary regularity. Its equality assertion is global and imposes no proximity to an ellipsoid.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Previous results and methods

One approach studies a body together with its second projection body. The class-reduction inequality $R_n(\Pi K)\le R_n(K)$, together with its equality condition, reduces the study of minimizers to bodies homothetic to $\Pi^2K$; see <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Saroglou2015](#ref-Saroglou2015), Sections 1–2]. Saroglou and Zvavitch proved a local minimum theorem under the hypothesis that the density of the surface-area measure, after an invertible linear change of variables, is sufficiently close to that of the ball in $L^\infty$ <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[SaroglouZvavitch2017](#ref-SaroglouZvavitch2017), Theorem 1.3]. Ivaki established local rigidity for $C^2$ solutions of $\Pi^2K=cK$ near the ball after an invertible linear change of variables <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Ivaki2018](#ref-Ivaki2018)]. These results use the harmonic structure of the projection operator and the special role of the directions arising from linear changes of coordinates.

Chen, Feng, Li, Xi, and Xu proved the unrestricted three-dimensional inequality, including ellipsoid equality <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026](#ref-ChenEtAl2026), Theorem 1.1]. Mielke-Sulz proved the conjecture for bodies of revolution in every dimension $n\ge3$ <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[MielkeSulz2026](#ref-MielkeSulz2026)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> treats arbitrary convex bodies in the remaining dimensions. Combining it with the separately proved three-dimensional theorem of Chen et al. establishes the conjecture for every $n\ge3$; that external theorem is not used in our proof for $n\ge4$.

Lutwak reformulated the conjecture as a lower bound for a two-body integral with kernel $|u\cdot v|$ against surface-area measures <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[LutwakPetty1990](#ref-LutwakPetty1990)]; see the explicit formulation in <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Saroglou2015](#ref-Saroglou2015), Introduction, item (i)]. Our reduction uses the same pairing for a broader class of positive measures satisfying a lower bound on integrals of support functions.

Our proof uses the classical first-variation and spherical-transform frameworks, but requires estimates and an affine normalization that apply to arbitrary bodies. The Wulff variations are closely related to the Aleksandrov variations for dual curvature measures developed by Huang, Lutwak, Yang, and Zhang <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HuangLutwakYangZhang2016](#ref-HuangLutwakYangZhang2016)]. The variational representation of a support functional belongs to the $L_p$ dual Minkowski framework; compare Huang and Zhao <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[HuangZhao2018](#ref-HuangZhao2018)]. We prove the needed representation directly, including its logarithmic endpoint. The cosine and Funk spectra are classical <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OlafssonPasqualeRubin2013](#ref-OlafssonPasqualeRubin2013)]; we derive their multipliers in our normalization. The further tasks are a strict estimate for all norms and a continuous choice of variational minimizers through changes of matrix rank. The latter permits a global choice of affine coordinates, where proximity to the ball is unavailable.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The argument

The proof passes from a body to a positive measure on vectors. After translating and normalizing $|K|=\kappa_n$, the first variation of volume gives a bounded, spanning probability measure $\eta_K$ on $\mathbb R^n$ such that $$\int h_M\,d\eta_K\ge (|M|/\kappa_n)^{1/n}$$ for every origin-symmetric convex body $M$. The same measure represents $h_{\Pi K}(y)$ as a fixed multiple of $\int|x\cdot y|\,d\eta_K(x)$. Theorem <a href="#thm:bilinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bilinear">19</a> bounds the pairing $$\iint|x\cdot y|\,d\eta(x)d\zeta(y)$$ for any two bounded, spanning positive measures satisfying this support-functional inequality. Applying the result to the measures of $K$ and its volume-normalized projection body yields the desired lower bound. Sharpness in the pairing returns equality to the homothety case of the first variation of volume.

Two ingredients have statements independent of projection bodies. Theorem <a href="#thm:norm-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:norm-estimate">11</a> bounds the higher spherical harmonics of a power of an arbitrary norm by a difference of its spherical means. The estimate is strict except for the Euclidean norm, and no smoothness is needed. Proposition <a href="#prop:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:representation">15</a> represents a positive support functional using the gradient of a variationally selected norm. A spherical sign test bounds the pairing below by a bilinear form involving the cosine and Funk transforms. The strict norm estimate controls the contribution of harmonics of degree at least four.

The degree-two component must be removed by choosing affine coordinates; it is not a zero eigenspace of the sign-test operator. Proposition <a href="#prop:position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:position">18</a> makes this choice by extending the family of variational minimizers continuously to singular matrices. At that boundary the gauges become seminorms. An explicit calculation in their missing directions gives an inward-pointing matrix field, and a fixed-point argument yields an invertible matrix at which the degree-two component vanishes. This position is needed for only one of the two measures. The other is transformed contragrediently, which preserves their pairing. These constructions and the strict norm estimate also supply the equality case.

Combining Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> with the separate three-dimensional result of Chen et al. gives several sharp inequalities in dimensions $n\ge3$. Lutwak’s implication yields Euclidean-ball lower bounds for lower-degree projection-body ratios, in which intrinsic volumes replace the volumes of shadows. Haddad’s $p=1$ implication bounds the integral of the absolute determinant of gradients below by the product of critical Lebesgue norms. Its diagonal case strengthens the Sobolev–Zhang affine $L^1$ inequality. The theorem also gives the sharp Holmes–Thompson isoperimetric inequality in normed spaces, where equality requires an ellipsoidal norm ball and a body homothetic to it. The full statements below distinguish the hypotheses and equality conclusions of these consequences.

Section <a href="#sec:gauges" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gauges">2</a> establishes the volume and gauge facts used in the reduction. Section <a href="#sec:spherical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:spherical">3</a> derives the sign test and its harmonic multipliers. Section <a href="#sec:norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:norm">4</a> proves the strict norm estimate, including the scalar inequalities in every dimension. Section <a href="#sec:position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:position">5</a> constructs the variational representation and affine position. Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a> proves the bilinear inequality and deduces Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">7</a> develops its consequences. We use the classical Brunn–Minkowski inequality with its homothety equality case and Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem in their stated forms; the first-variation reduction, harmonic estimates, variational construction, and equality argument are proved below.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Notation

Throughout Sections <a href="#sec:gauges" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gauges">2</a>–<a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">6</a>, $n\ge4$, $p=n-1$, and $\sigma$ is uniform probability on $S^{n-1}$. The symbol $\mathbb E_{\sigma}$ denotes integration against $\sigma$, and $$b_n=\mathbb E_{\sigma}|u_1|.$$ Repeated spherical integrals use independent directions. A symmetric body always means an origin-symmetric body. When a function on the sphere is used as a degree-one homogeneous test on vectors, it is extended by positive homogeneity and given value zero at the origin. Euclidean gradients are denoted by $\nabla$; the tangential gradient and Laplacian are $\nabla_S$ and $\Delta_S$.

<a id="sec:gauges"></a>
### Gauges and projection measures

We first associate a measure to an arbitrary convex body. A volume variation will give both the lower bound satisfied by this measure and its relation to the projection body. No symmetry of the original body is needed.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Differentiating a gauge

For a convex body $L$ containing the origin in its interior, its *gauge* is $$g_L(x)=\inf\{a>0:x\in aL\}.$$ It is convex and positively homogeneous, and $L=\{x:g_L(x)\le1\}$. When $L$ is origin-symmetric, its gauge is a norm. A continuous function $\phi$ on $S^{n-1}$, when evaluated at a vector, will mean its positively homogeneous extension $$\phi(x)=|x|\phi(x/|x|)\quad(x\ne0),\qquad \phi(0)=0.$$ This convention does not impose evenness on $\phi$.

Varying the supporting constraints in this way is a Wulff variation. The almost-everywhere radial differentiation below is the gauge form of the Aleksandrov variation used in dual Brunn–Minkowski theory; compare <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[HuangLutwakYangZhang2016](#ref-HuangLutwakYangZhang2016), Lemmas 4.1–4.3 and Corollary 4.9]. We give the argument directly because the later minimization uses arbitrary continuous constraints of both signs, without boundary regularity.

<a id="stmt-lem-gauge-variation"></a>
<div id="lem:gauge-variation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gauge-formulas"></a>
<a id="eq:gauge-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:constraint-volume-derivative"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Gauge variation). *Let $s:S^{n-1}\to(0,\infty)$ and $\phi:S^{n-1}\to\mathbb R$ be continuous. For sufficiently small $|t|$, define $$W_t=\bigcap_{v\in S^{n-1}}\{x\in\mathbb R^n:x\cdot v\le s(v)+t\phi(v)\},
 \qquad g_t=g_{W_t}.$$ Then $W_t$ is a convex body containing the origin in its interior, and $$\label{eq:gauge-formulas}
 g_t(x)=\max_{v\in S^{n-1}}\frac{x\cdot v}{s(v)+t\phi(v)},
 \qquad
 \frac{|W_t|}{\kappa_n}=\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_t(u)^{-n}.$$ For almost every $u\in S^{n-1}$, the maximizing vector $v/s(v)$ at $t=0$ is unique and equals the Euclidean gradient $\nabla g_0(u)$. At these directions, $$\label{eq:gauge-derivative}
 \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}g_t(u)
 =-g_0(u)\phi\bigl(\nabla g_0(u)\bigr).$$ On the sphere the gauges are uniformly bounded above and away from zero for small $|t|$, and the difference quotients $(g_t-g_0)/t$ are uniformly bounded. In particular, $$\label{eq:constraint-volume-derivative}
 \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}|W_t|
 =n\kappa_n\mathbb E_{\sigma}\!\left[
   g_0(u)^{-n}\phi\bigl(\nabla g_0(u)\bigr)\right].$$ For $s=h_L$, one has $W_0=L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gauge-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gauge-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $0<m\le s(v)\le M$. For small $|t|$ the constraints $s+t\phi$ lie between $m/2$ and some fixed finite constant $M'$. Consequently $$(m/2)B_2^n\subset W_t\subset M'B_2^n.$$ The defining inequalities show that $x\in aW_t$ precisely when $x\cdot v\le a(s(v)+t\phi(v))$ for every $v$. Taking the least such $a$ gives the maximum formula in <a href="#eq:gauge-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauge-formulas">[eq:gauge-formulas]</a>. The radial function of $W_t$ is $1/g_t(u)$, so polar integration, with spherical area $n\kappa_n$, gives the volume formula. If $s=h_L$, separation identifies the defining intersection with $L$.

For completeness, the maximum formula also gives the differentiability needed here without any regularity assumption on the boundary of $W_0$. The function $g_0$ is convex and Lipschitz: it is the maximum of linear functions whose coefficient vectors $v/s(v)$ form a compact set. On every coordinate line, a finite convex function has a derivative except on a set of one-dimensional measure zero. Slicing therefore shows that all coordinate partial derivatives of $g_0$ exist at almost every point of $\mathbb R^n$. If a vector $w=v/s(v)$ attains the maximum at such a point $x$, then $$g_0(x+he_i)\ge g_0(x)+h w_i\qquad(h\in\mathbb R).$$ The two signs of $h$ imply $w_i=\partial_i g_0(x)$ for every $i$. Thus the maximizing vector is unique.

Uniqueness implies differentiability. Indeed, if $w_z$ maximizes at $x+z$ and $w$ maximizes at $x$, compactness and continuity imply $w_z\to w$ as $z\to0$. The maximum formula gives $$0\le g_0(x+z)-g_0(x)-w\cdot z
 \le (w_z-w)\cdot z=o(|z|).$$ The nondifferentiability set away from the origin is invariant under positive dilations, by homogeneity. Since it has Lebesgue measure zero, polar integration shows that its intersection with $S^{n-1}$ has $\sigma$-measure zero.

Fix a differentiability direction $u$, and let $v_0$ maximize at $t=0$. Uniqueness of $v_0/s(v_0)$ implies uniqueness of $v_0$ itself, since $s$ is positive. Every maximizing direction $v_t$ for $g_t(u)$ tends to $v_0$ as $t\to0$. The derivatives with respect to $t$ of the functions $$(u,v,t)\longmapsto\frac{u\cdot v}{s(v)+t\phi(v)}$$ are continuous on the relevant compact set. Comparing the maxima at $t$ and $0$, using $v_t$ and $v_0$, therefore gives $$\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0}g_t(u)
 =-\frac{(u\cdot v_0)\phi(v_0)}{s(v_0)^2}
 =-g_0(u)\phi\!\left(\frac{v_0}{s(v_0)}\right).$$ This is <a href="#eq:gauge-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauge-derivative">[eq:gauge-derivative]</a>. Notice that the argument uses neither convexity nor evenness of the function $s+t\phi$.

The same uniform bound on the derivatives of the displayed quotients bounds $(g_t-g_0)/t$ uniformly in $u$. The ball inclusions bound $g_t$ above and away from zero on $S^{n-1}$. Dominated convergence now differentiates the volume formula and proves <a href="#eq:constraint-volume-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:constraint-volume-derivative">[eq:constraint-volume-derivative]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### A volume inequality with its equality case

We use the classical Brunn–Minkowski inequality, including its equality case, in the following form; see <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Gardner2002](#ref-Gardner2002), Sections 3 and 5]. The first-variation statement and its equality consequence are then proved in the gauge coordinates needed for our measures.

<a id="stmt-thm-brunn-minkowski"></a>
<div id="thm:brunn-minkowski" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Brunn–Minkowski inequality). *For every integer $d\ge1$ and convex bodies $A,B\subset\mathbb R^d$, write $|\cdot|$ for $d$-dimensional volume. For $0\le t\le1$, <a id="eq:brunn-minkowski"></a>$$\label{eq:brunn-minkowski}
 |(1-t)A+tB|^{1/d}
 \ge (1-t)|A|^{1/d}+t|B|^{1/d}.$$ If $0<t<1$, equality holds if and only if $B=a+cA$ for some $a\in\mathbb R^d$ and $c>0$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-volume-first-variation"></a>
<div id="cor:volume-first-variation" class="corollary">

**Corollary 4** (First variation of volume). *Let $K,M\subset\mathbb R^n$ be convex bodies, with the origin interior to $K$. Then <a id="eq:minkowski-volume-derivative"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0+}|K+tM|
 &=n\kappa_n\mathbb E_{\sigma}\!\left[g_K(u)^{-n}
                 h_M\bigl(\nabla g_K(u)\bigr)\right]
                 \label{eq:minkowski-volume-derivative}\\
 &\ge n|K|^{(n-1)/n}|M|^{1/n}.\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Equality in the inequality holds if and only if $K$ and $M$ are homothetic up to translation. The derivative identity remains valid when $M$ is an arbitrary nonempty compact convex set.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-volume-first-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-volume-first-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Support functions add under Minkowski addition, so Lemma <a href="#lem:gauge-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gauge-variation">2</a>, with $s=h_K$ and $\phi=h_M$, gives the derivative identity. This applies equally to a compact convex set $M$ of lower dimension. For full-dimensional $M$, Theorem <a href="#thm:brunn-minkowski" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:brunn-minkowski">3</a> and homogeneity give $$F(t):=|K+tM|^{1/n}\ge |K|^{1/n}+t|M|^{1/n}
 \qquad(t\ge0).$$ In addition, $F$ is concave, by applying that theorem to $K+sM$ and $K+tM$. Its right derivative at zero therefore satisfies $F'(0+)\ge |M|^{1/n}$, which is the asserted lower bound.

If equality holds in this derivative bound, concavity gives $$F(t)\le F(0)+tF'(0+)=|K|^{1/n}+t|M|^{1/n}.$$ Thus the preceding volume inequality is an equality for every $t>0$. The equality case of Theorem <a href="#thm:brunn-minkowski" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:brunn-minkowski">3</a>, applied for example to $K+M$, forces homothety. Conversely, if $M=a+cK$ with $c>0$, then $K+tM=ta+(1+tc)K$, which verifies equality in the derivative bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The measure associated with a convex body

The construction below is a gauge-coordinate form of the classical surface-area and mixed-volume description of projection bodies; see <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Gardner2002](#ref-Gardner2002), Section 6] and <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Saroglou2015](#ref-Saroglou2015), Section 1]. The local segment calculation will fix its normalization and retain the equality information from the first variation.

For a finite positive Borel measure $\eta$ on $\mathbb R^n$ with bounded support, write $$\tau_\eta(M)=\int h_M(x)\,d\eta(x).$$

<a id="stmt-def-admissible-measure"></a>
<div id="def:admissible-measure" class="definition">

<a id="eq:functional-condition"></a>
**Definition 5**. We call $\eta$ *admissible* if its support spans $\mathbb R^n$ and $$\label{eq:functional-condition}
 \tau_\eta(M)\ge\left(\frac{|M|}{\kappa_n}\right)^{1/n}
 \quad\text{for every origin-symmetric convex body }M\subset\mathbb R^n.$$ The measure itself is not required to be symmetric.

</div>

<a id="stmt-prop-projection-measure"></a>
<div id="prop:projection-measure" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:eta-definition"></a>
<a id="eq:projection-measure"></a>
<a id="eq:eta-normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:ball-constant"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Projection measure). *Translate and dilate a convex body $K$ so that $0\in\operatorname{int}K$ and $|K|=\kappa_n$. Define $\eta_K$ by $$\label{eq:eta-definition}
 \int \psi(x)\,d\eta_K(x)
 =\mathbb E_{\sigma}\!\left[g_K(u)^{-n}\psi\bigl(\nabla g_K(u)\bigr)\right]$$ for nonnegative Borel functions $\psi$; the choice of the gradient on its null exceptional set is immaterial. Then $\eta_K$ is an admissible probability measure of bounded support. Equality in <a href="#eq:functional-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-condition">[eq:functional-condition]</a> for $\eta_K$ and a symmetric body $M$ holds if and only if $K$ is a translate of a positive dilate of $M$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:projection-measure}
 h_{\Pi K}(y)=\frac{n\kappa_n}{2}
                  \int |x\cdot y|\,d\eta_K(x)
 \qquad(y\in\mathbb R^n),$$ and $$\label{eq:eta-normalization}
 \int h_K(x)\,d\eta_K(x)=1.$$ For the unit ball, $\eta_{B_2^n}=\sigma$, and consequently $$\label{eq:ball-constant}
 \frac{n\kappa_n b_n}{2}=\kappa_{n-1}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-projection-measure"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-projection-measure</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#eq:gauge-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauge-formulas">[eq:gauge-formulas]</a>, $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_K^{-n}=1$, so <a href="#eq:eta-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eta-definition">[eq:eta-definition]</a> defines a probability measure. The active vectors $v/h_K(v)$ lie in a bounded set because $h_K$ is positive on the sphere. Hence $\eta_K$ has bounded support. Applying Corollary <a href="#cor:volume-first-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:volume-first-variation">4</a> gives, for every symmetric convex body $M$, $$\tau_{\eta_K}(M)
 =\frac1{n\kappa_n}
        \left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0+}|K+tM|
 \ge\left(\frac{|M|}{\kappa_n}\right)^{1/n},$$ with precisely the stated homothety equality condition. The same derivative identity with $M=K$, using $|K+tK|=(1+t)^n\kappa_n$, proves <a href="#eq:eta-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:eta-normalization">[eq:eta-normalization]</a>; this identity does not require $K$ to be symmetric.

Now let $y\ne0$ and take $M=[-y,y]$. Every nonempty fiber of $K$ parallel to $y$ is a compact interval. Adding $t[-y,y]$ increases its length by $2t|y|$, without changing its projection onto $y^\perp$. Fubini’s theorem therefore gives $$|K+t[-y,y]|=|K|+
     2t|y|\operatorname{vol}_{n-1}
                   (\operatorname{proj}_{y^\perp}K)
 =|K|+2t h_{\Pi K}(y).$$ Since the support function of the segment is $x\mapsto|x\cdot y|$, the derivative identity yields <a href="#eq:projection-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-measure">[eq:projection-measure]</a>. The formula is also true at $y=0$. The projection of a full-dimensional convex body has positive $(n-1)$-volume in every direction. If $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta_K$ failed to span $\mathbb R^n$, a nonzero vector perpendicular to its span would make the integral in <a href="#eq:projection-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-measure">[eq:projection-measure]</a> vanish. This contradiction proves the spanning property and hence admissibility.

Finally, for $K=B_2^n$ one has $g_K(u)=1$ and $\nabla g_K(u)=u$ on the sphere, so $\eta_K=\sigma$. Its projection volume in every unit direction is $\kappa_{n-1}$. Substituting this into <a href="#eq:projection-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-measure">[eq:projection-measure]</a> proves <a href="#eq:ball-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-constant">[eq:ball-constant]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:spherical"></a>
### A spherical sign estimate

The estimate in this section separates the constant part of a spherical function from its harmonics of degree at least four. The degree-two part will later be removed by a linear change of coordinates. Throughout, $p=n-1$, surface measure $\sigma$ has total mass one, and inner products and norms of functions are those of $L^2(\sigma)$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Harmonics and the spectral gap

Let $\mathcal H_l$ be the restrictions to $S^{n-1}$ of homogeneous harmonic polynomials of degree $l$, and put $$\lambda_l=l(l+n-2)=l(l+p-1).$$ For an even function, let $Q$ denote orthogonal projection onto the closed sum of $\mathcal H_4,\mathcal H_6,\ldots$.

<a id="stmt-lem-spherical-harmonics"></a>
<div id="lem:spherical-harmonics" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:spectral-gap"></a>
**Lemma 7**. *The spaces $\mathcal H_l$ form an orthogonal decomposition of $L^2(\sigma)$, and $\Delta_SY=-\lambda_lY$ for $Y\in\mathcal H_l$. Every even $H\in C^1(S^{n-1})$ satisfies $$\label{eq:spectral-gap}
 \lambda_2\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(H)+(\lambda_4-\lambda_2)\left\lVert QH\right\rVert_2^2
 \le \mathbb E_{\sigma}\left|\nabla_SH\right|^2,
 \qquad \lambda_2=2n,\quad \lambda_4=4(n+2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spherical-harmonics"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spherical-harmonics</summary>

*Proof.* The polar-coordinate formula for the Euclidean Laplacian gives $$\Delta_{\mathbb R^n}\big(r^lY(u)\big)
 =r^{l-2}\big(l(l+n-2)Y(u)+\Delta_SY(u)\big).$$ This proves the eigenvalue formula. Integration by parts on the sphere then proves orthogonality of spaces with distinct degrees.

We give the completeness argument explicitly. If $Y_l$ is homogeneous and harmonic, direct differentiation yields $$\Delta_{\mathbb R^n}\big(\left|x\right|^{2j}Y_l(x)\big)
 =2j(2l+2j+n-2)\left|x\right|^{2j-2}Y_l(x).$$ Induction on the degree now decomposes every homogeneous polynomial into terms $\left|x\right|^{2j}Y_l(x)$. Indeed, decompose its Laplacian by the induction hypothesis and use the displayed identity to find a sum of such terms with the same Laplacian. The difference is harmonic. Restricting to the unit sphere shows that every polynomial restriction belongs to the span of the $\mathcal H_l$.

Polynomial restrictions uniformly approximate continuous functions on $S^{n-1}$. To see this without an approximation theorem, set $$K_j(u,v)=
 \frac{((1+u\cdot v)/2)^j}
 {\displaystyle\int_{S^{n-1}}((1+u\cdot w)/2)^j\,d\sigma(w)}.$$ The denominator is positive and independent of $u$, so $T_jH(u)=\int K_j(u,v)H(v)\,d\sigma(v)$ is a polynomial in $u$. For $0<\delta<2$, let $m_\delta>0$ be the measure of the cap $\{v:\left|u-v\right|<\delta/2\}$. Since $(1+u\cdot v)/2=1-\left|u-v\right|^2/4$, $$\int_{\left|u-v\right|\ge\delta}K_j(u,v)\,d\sigma(v)
 \le \frac1{m_\delta}
 \left(\frac{1-\delta^2/4}{1-\delta^2/16}\right)^j.$$ Uniform continuity of $H$ therefore gives $T_jH\to H$ uniformly. Continuous functions are dense in $L^2(\sigma)$: approximate the sets in a simple function from inside by compact sets and from outside by open sets, then interpolate their indicators continuously using distance to these sets. The squared error is bounded by the measure of the intervening sets. This proves completeness.

Choose real orthonormal bases $Y_{l,a}$ of the spaces $\mathcal H_l$, with $Y_{0,1}=1$. Integration by parts shows that the tangent fields $\nabla_SY_{l,a}/\sqrt{\lambda_l}$, for $l\ge1$, are orthonormal. Bessel’s inequality, which follows by expanding the squared distance from any finite orthogonal sum, consequently gives $$\mathbb E_{\sigma}\left|\nabla_SH\right|^2
 \ge \sum_{l\ge1,a}
 \left|\mathbb E_{\sigma}\,\nabla_SH\cdot
       \frac{\nabla_SY_{l,a}}{\sqrt{\lambda_l}}\right|^2
 =\sum_{l\ge1,a}\lambda_l\left|\mathbb E_{\sigma}(HY_{l,a})\right|^2.$$ No differentiation of an infinite series is needed. If $H$ is even, its odd-degree coefficients vanish. Completeness identifies its variance with the sum of the squared nonconstant coefficients and $\left\lVert QH\right\rVert_2^2$ with the corresponding sum over even $l\ge4$. Using $\lambda_l\ge\lambda_2$ for even $l\ge2$ and $\lambda_l\ge\lambda_4$ for $l\ge4$ proves <a href="#eq:spectral-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectral-gap">[eq:spectral-gap]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The sign-test operator

<a id="eq:vector-field"></a>
<a id="eq:sign-form"></a>
For $k>0$ and an even differentiable function $f$ on $S^{n-1}$, define $$\label{eq:vector-field}
 X_f(u)=f(u)u+k^{-1}\nabla_Sf(u).$$ For even $C^1$ functions $f_1,f_2$, set $$\label{eq:sign-form}
 \mathcal B(f_1,f_2)
 =\frac1{b_n}\iint_{S^{n-1}\times S^{n-1}}
   \mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(u\cdot v)\,X_{f_1}(u)\cdot X_{f_2}(v)
   \,d\sigma(u)d\sigma(v).$$ The value assigned to $\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(0)$ is immaterial. Since $|a|\ge\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(u\cdot v)a$ for every real $a$, the form gives the lower bound $$\iint |X_{f_1}(u)\cdot X_{f_2}(v)|\,d\sigma(u)d\sigma(v)
 \ge b_n\mathcal B(f_1,f_2).$$ We estimate this lower bound using two spherical transforms. Write $\sigma_{u^\perp}$ for uniform probability on the equator $u^\perp\cap S^{n-1}$, and define $$(Cf)(u)=\frac1{b_n}\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|u\cdot v\right|f(v)\,d\sigma(v),
 \qquad
 (Pf)(u)=\int_{u^\perp\cap S^{n-1}}f(v)\,d\sigma_{u^\perp}(v).$$ These are the cosine and Funk transforms, here normalized to preserve constants; compare <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[OlafssonPasqualeRubin2013](#ref-OlafssonPasqualeRubin2013), Section 2, especially (2.3), (2.5)–(2.6)]. The local multiplier calculation below uses this probability-measure convention throughout. Both are self-adjoint contractions on $L^2(\sigma)$ and preserve constants. For $C$ this follows from symmetry of its nonnegative kernel and Jensen’s inequality. For $P$, average first over the band $\left|u\cdot v\right|<\epsilon$, dividing by its measure. The resulting kernel is symmetric and preserves constants. Spherical coordinates show that, for continuous $f$, these band averages tend to $Pf$; Jensen’s inequality and passage to the limit give the same conclusions for $P$, followed by extension to $L^2(\sigma)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-sign-operator"></a>
<div id="lem:sign-operator" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sign-operator"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *For even $C^1$ functions, $$\label{eq:sign-operator}
 \mathcal B(f_1,f_2)
 =\left\langle f_1,
   \left[C+\left(\frac{2p}{k}+\frac{p^2}{k^2}\right)(C-P)\right]
   f_2\right\rangle.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sign-operator"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sign-operator</summary>

<a id="eq:hemisphere-ibp"></a>
*Proof.* Let $d_0$ be the density at zero of $u\cdot v$ for uniform $u$ and fixed $v\in S^{n-1}$. Equivalently, $d_0$ is the ratio of the surface areas of $S^{n-2}$ and $S^{n-1}$. For a fixed ambient vector $w$, its tangential projection has divergence $-p\,u\cdot w$. Integrating separately on the two hemispheres gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_{S^{n-1}}\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(u\cdot v)\,\nabla_Sf(u)\cdot w\,d\sigma(u)
 \notag\\
 &\quad=p\int_{S^{n-1}}\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(u\cdot v)f(u)\,u\cdot w\,d\sigma(u)
 -2d_0\int_{v^\perp\cap S^{n-1}}f(u)\,v\cdot w
       \,d\sigma_{v^\perp}(u).
 \label{eq:hemisphere-ibp}
\end{aligned}$$ The outward conormals on the equator are $-v$ and $v$, respectively; the sign change between the hemispheres makes their contributions add with the displayed negative sign. Taking $f=1$ and $w=v$ yields $$2d_0=p b_n.$$

The radial–radial term in <a href="#eq:sign-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-form">[eq:sign-form]</a>, before division by $b_n$, is $b_n\langle f_1,Cf_2\rangle$. Apply <a href="#eq:hemisphere-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hemisphere-ibp">[eq:hemisphere-ibp]</a> with $w=v$ and then integrate against $f_2(v)$. Each of the two mixed radial–gradient terms, before its factor $k^{-1}$, equals $$p b_n\langle f_1,(C-P)f_2\rangle.$$ For the gradient–gradient term, first integrate in $u$ with $w=\nabla_Sf_2(v)$. Its equator term vanishes because $v\cdot\nabla_Sf_2(v)=0$. What remains is $$p\iint f_1(u)\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(u\cdot v)\,
      u\cdot\nabla_Sf_2(v)\,d\sigma(u)d\sigma(v).$$ A second application of <a href="#eq:hemisphere-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hemisphere-ibp">[eq:hemisphere-ibp]</a>, now in $v$ with $w=u$, makes this $p^2b_n\langle f_1,(C-P)f_2\rangle$. Adding the four terms proves <a href="#eq:sign-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-operator">[eq:sign-operator]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Multipliers and passage to norms

<a id="stmt-lem-spherical-multipliers"></a>
<div id="lem:spherical-multipliers" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:transform-multipliers"></a>
<a id="eq:cosine-recurrence"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *On $\mathcal H_{2j}$ the operators $P$ and $C$ are scalar multipliers, with $$\label{eq:transform-multipliers}
 P_{2j}=(-1)^j
 \frac{1\cdot3\cdots(2j-1)}{p(p+2)\cdots(p+2j-2)},
 \qquad
 C_{2j}=\frac{pP_{2j}}{p-\lambda_{2j}},$$ where empty products equal one. In particular, $$\label{eq:cosine-recurrence}
 \begin{gathered}
 C_0=1,\qquad C_2=\frac1{p+2},\qquad
 C_4=-\frac1{(p+2)(p+4)},\\[3pt]
 \frac{C_{l+2}}{C_l}=-\frac{l-1}{l+p+2}\quad(l\ge2\text{ even}).
 \end{gathered}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spherical-multipliers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spherical-multipliers</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $u\in S^{n-1}$ and a homogeneous harmonic polynomial $Y_{2j}$. Let $Z$ be a standard Gaussian in the $p$-dimensional space $u^\perp$. Its direction is uniform on the equator and independent of its radius, so polar integration gives $$\mathbb E Y_{2j}(Z)
 =p(p+2)\cdots(p+2j-2)\,(PY_{2j})(u).$$ The radial moment here follows by successive integration by parts in $\int_0^\infty r^{p+2j-1}e^{-r^2/2}\,dr$. The one-dimensional Gaussian moment recurrence $\mathbb E Z_1^{2a}=(2a-1)\mathbb E Z_1^{2a-2}$, together with independence of coordinates, also gives $$\mathbb E Y_{2j}(Z)
 =\frac{(\Delta_{u^\perp})^jY_{2j}(0)}{2^j j!}.$$ This identity follows term by term on monomials; monomials with an odd exponent have both sides zero, and the other coefficients are exactly the multinomial coefficients in the power of the Laplacian. Harmonicity and commutation of constant-coefficient derivatives imply $$(\Delta_{u^\perp})^jY_{2j}
   =(-1)^j\partial_u^{2j}Y_{2j},
 \qquad
 \partial_u^{2j}Y_{2j}(0)=(2j)!Y_{2j}(u).$$ Since $(2j)!/(2^j j!)=1\cdot3\cdots(2j-1)$, the formula for $P_{2j}$ follows.

To obtain $C$, integrate by parts on the two hemispheres in the variable $v$. Off the equator, $\Delta_S\left|u\cdot v\right|=-p\left|u\cdot v\right|$; the jump of its normal derivative contributes $2d_0$ times equator averaging. Thus, for any smooth $Y$, $$\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|u\cdot v\right|\Delta_SY(v)\,d\sigma(v)
 =-p\int_{S^{n-1}}\left|u\cdot v\right|Y(v)\,d\sigma(v)
   +2d_0(PY)(u).$$ For $Y\in\mathcal H_l$, division by $b_n$ gives $-\lambda_l CY=p(PY-CY)$. The denominator $p-\lambda_l=-(l-1)(l+p)$ is nonzero for every even $l$, proving the second formula in <a href="#eq:transform-multipliers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transform-multipliers">[eq:transform-multipliers]</a>. Its first three values give those in <a href="#eq:cosine-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cosine-recurrence">[eq:cosine-recurrence]</a>; division of successive values, using $P_{l+2}/P_l=-(l+1)/(l+p)$, gives the recurrence. ◻

</details>

Set <a id="eq:tail-constant"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-constant}
 c_{n,k}=
 \frac{1+\left(2/k+(n-1)/k^2\right)4(n+2)}{(n+1)(n+3)}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-sign-estimate"></a>
<div id="prop:sign-estimate" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:sign-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:norm-vector-field"></a>
**Proposition 10**. *Let $k>0$ and let $f_1,f_2$ be even $C^1$ functions on $S^{n-1}$. If $f_1$ has zero projection onto $\mathcal H_2$, then $$\label{eq:sign-bound}
 \mathcal B(f_1,f_2)
 \ge (\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_1)(\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_2)
       -c_{n,k}\left\lVert Qf_1\right\rVert_2\left\lVert Qf_2\right\rVert_2.$$ The operator identity <a href="#eq:sign-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-operator">[eq:sign-operator]</a> and this estimate also hold when $f_i=g_i^k$ for arbitrary norms $g_i$ on $\mathbb R^n$, with the vector fields defined almost everywhere. In that case $$\label{eq:norm-vector-field}
 X_{g_i^k}(u)=g_i(u)^{k-1}\nabla g_i(u).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-sign-estimate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-sign-estimate</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:spherical-multipliers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spherical-multipliers">9</a>, the operator in <a href="#eq:sign-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-operator">[eq:sign-operator]</a> has multiplier one on constants and, on degree $l$, multiplier $$\mu_l=C_l\left[1+\left(\frac2k+\frac p{k^2}\right)\lambda_l\right].$$ Indeed, $C_l-P_l=(\lambda_l/p)C_l$. Put $a=2/k+p/k^2>0$. For even $l\ge4$, $$(l+p+2)\lambda_l-(l-1)\lambda_{l+2}
 =(p-1)l^2+(p^2-1)l+2(p+1)>0.$$ Since also $l-1<l+p+2$, it follows that $$\frac{\left|\mu_{l+2}\right|}{\left|\mu_l\right|}
 =\frac{l-1}{l+p+2}\,
   \frac{1+a\lambda_{l+2}}{1+a\lambda_l}\le1.$$ Consequently $\left|\mu_l\right|\le\left|\mu_4\right|=c_{n,k}$ on all even $l\ge4$. The constant term of the bilinear form is $(\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_1)(\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_2)$, and its degree-two term vanishes by hypothesis. Cauchy–Schwarz on the remaining harmonic coefficients proves <a href="#eq:sign-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-bound">[eq:sign-bound]</a>. This expansion is justified by the completeness in Lemma <a href="#lem:spherical-harmonics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spherical-harmonics">7</a> and the boundedness of $C$ and $P$.

We finish by proving the passage to norms, retaining the details needed when their unit balls have corners. Let $g$ be a norm and let $g_\epsilon=g*\rho_\epsilon$ on $\mathbb R^n$, where $\rho_\epsilon$ is a smooth, even, nonnegative function of integral one, supported in the ball of radius $\epsilon$. The functions $g_\epsilon$ are smooth, even and convex. If $L$ is the Euclidean Lipschitz constant of $g$, then $$\left|g_\epsilon-g\right|\le L\epsilon,
 \qquad \left|\nabla g_\epsilon\right|\le L.$$ At a differentiability point $x$ of $g$, these gradients converge to $\nabla g(x)$. Indeed, every convergent subsequence of the bounded gradients has a limit $a$, and convexity gives, on passing to the limit, $$g(y)\ge g(x)+a\cdot(y-x)\qquad(y\in\mathbb R^n).$$ Taking $y=x\pm th$ and then $t\downarrow0$ forces $a\cdot h=\nabla g(x)\cdot h$ for every $h$, so the only possible limit is $\nabla g(x)$.

Lemma <a href="#lem:gauge-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gauge-variation">2</a> supplies differentiability of $g$ at almost every spherical direction. Equivalently, one may use its almost-everywhere differentiability in $\mathbb R^n$ and homogeneity: the exceptional set is a union of rays, so polar integration makes its intersection with the sphere null. On the sphere $g$ is bounded away from zero. Thus the restrictions $f_\epsilon=g_\epsilon^k|_{S^{n-1}}$ converge uniformly to $g^k$, and $$\nabla_Sf_\epsilon(u)
 =k g_\epsilon(u)^{k-1}
   (I_n-uu^t)\nabla g_\epsilon(u)
 \longrightarrow
 k g(u)^{k-1}(I_n-uu^t)\nabla g(u)$$ almost everywhere and in $L^2(\sigma)$, by bounded convergence. Euler’s identity $u\cdot\nabla g(u)=g(u)$, obtained by differentiating $g(tu)=tg(u)$, identifies the limiting vector field with <a href="#eq:norm-vector-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-vector-field">[eq:norm-vector-field]</a>.

The approximants $g_\epsilon$ need not be homogeneous; only their restrictions and tangential gradients are used here. The fields $X_{f_\epsilon}$ converge in $L^2$, so the bounded sign kernel in <a href="#eq:sign-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-form">[eq:sign-form]</a> permits passage to the limit. The right side of <a href="#eq:sign-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-operator">[eq:sign-operator]</a> also converges, because $C$ and $P$ are bounded on $L^2$. This proves the operator identity for norm powers. Finally apply its harmonic multiplier estimate directly to the limiting functions. The approximants themselves need not have vanishing degree-two part: that hypothesis is used only for the limiting $f_1$. This proves <a href="#eq:sign-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sign-bound">[eq:sign-bound]</a> in the stated nonsmooth setting. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:norm"></a>
### A strict inequality for norms

<a id="eq:k-choice"></a>
We now bound the higher spherical harmonics of a power of a norm by two of its means. Convexity supplies the differential estimate; the remaining step consists of scalar inequalities. Throughout this section set $$\label{eq:k-choice}
 k=\begin{cases}1,&n=4,\\2,&n\ge5,\end{cases}
 \qquad c=c_{n,k},\qquad d_* =\lambda_4-\lambda_2=2n+8.$$ Thus the first power is used in dimension four and the square in higher dimensions. The scalar estimates below establish these two choices with the same strict conclusion.

<a id="stmt-thm-norm-estimate"></a>
<div id="thm:norm-estimate" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:norm-estimate"></a>
**Theorem 11**. *Let $g$ be a norm on $\mathbb R^n$, where $n\ge4$, normalized by $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{-n}=1$ on $S^{n-1}$. Then $$\label{eq:norm-estimate}
 c_{n,k}\|Q(g^k)\|_2^2
 \le (\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^k)^2-(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1})^2.$$ The inequality is strict unless $g=1$ on $S^{n-1}$.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Convexity and a scalar decomposition

<a id="eq:scalar-basic"></a>
For $z>0$ define $$\begin{aligned}
 e(z)&=\frac{z^{-n}-1}{n},&
 M(z)&=z^k-1+k e(z),&
 W(z)&=z^k-z^{k-1}+e(z).\label{eq:scalar-basic}
\end{aligned}$$ Since $M'(z)=k(z^{k-1}-z^{-n-1})$, the function $M$ decreases up to $1$, increases thereafter, and satisfies $M(1)=M'(1)=0$. In particular, $M\ge0$. For a norm with $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{-n}=1$, the correction $e(g)$ has mean zero. We will split $g^k-1$ into a term controlled by convexity and a remainder controlled by its variance. Choose the constants <a id="eq:scalar-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:scalar-constants}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 n&\beta&w\\ \hline
 4&1/2&3/14\\
 5,6&2/3&1/3\\
 n\ge7&2/3&1/2
\end{array}$$ and split <a id="eq:scalar-split"></a>$$\label{eq:scalar-split}
 G(z)=\beta M(z)\mathbf 1_{\{z>1\}},\qquad
 D(z)=z^k-1-G(z).$$ Thus $G$ vanishes for $z\le1$, while $D$ retains the remaining part of $z^k-1$. To estimate $G(g)$ by convexity, let $I(z)=0$ for $0<z\le1$, and, for $z>1$, let <a id="eq:scalar-I"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 I(z)&=\int_1^z M'(a)^2\,da\notag\\
 &=k^2\left(\frac{z^{2k-1}-1}{2k-1}
 -\frac{2(1-z^{k-1-n})}{n+1-k}
 +\frac{1-z^{-2n-1}}{2n+1}\right).
 \label{eq:scalar-I}
\end{aligned}$$ Both $G$ and $I$ are continuously differentiable across $1$; $I\ge0$ and $G'^2=\beta^2 I'$.

<a id="stmt-lem-curvature"></a>
<div id="lem:curvature" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:norm-curvature"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *For every norm $g$ on $\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{eq:norm-curvature}
 2n\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G(g))+d_*\|QG(g)\|_2^2
 \le p\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[gI(g)],\qquad p=n-1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that $g$ is smooth away from the origin. The trace of its Euclidean Hessian on the tangent space at $u\in S^{n-1}$ is $\Delta_Sg(u)+p g(u)$, and it is nonnegative by convexity. Multiplying by $I(g)\ge0$ and integrating by parts gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{\sigma}|\nabla_SG(g)|^2
 &=\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[I'(g)|\nabla_Sg|^2]
 =-\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[I(g)\Delta_Sg]
 \le p\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[gI(g)].
\end{aligned}$$ The spectral gap estimate <a href="#eq:spectral-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectral-gap">[eq:spectral-gap]</a> proves <a href="#eq:norm-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-curvature">[eq:norm-curvature]</a> in this case.

Here is a passage to arbitrary norms that does not assume the approximants are homogeneous. Convolve the ambient norm with smooth, even, nonnegative mollifiers of mass one and shrinking compact support, and write the resulting smooth convex functions as $g_\varepsilon$. On the unit sphere their tangent Hessian trace is $$\Delta_Sg_\varepsilon+p\partial_r g_\varepsilon\ge0.$$ The preceding argument and <a href="#eq:spectral-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectral-gap">[eq:spectral-gap]</a> therefore give $$2n\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G(g_\varepsilon))+d_*\|QG(g_\varepsilon)\|_2^2
 \le p\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[I(g_\varepsilon)\partial_r g_\varepsilon].$$ The functions $g_\varepsilon$ converge uniformly to $g$ on compact sets, are uniformly Lipschitz there, and their gradients converge to $\nabla g$ at every differentiability point of $g$. To see the last assertion, every limit of their bounded gradients satisfies the subgradient inequality for $g$ by convexity and uniform convergence; differentiability makes that subgradient unique. The exceptional directions have spherical measure zero, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:gauge-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gauge-variation">2</a>. Homogeneity of $g$ then gives $\partial_r g_\varepsilon(u)\to\nabla g(u)\cdot u=g(u)$ almost everywhere. On the sphere the approximants stay in a fixed positive compact interval, while their radial derivatives are bounded. Uniform convergence on the left and dominated convergence on the right prove <a href="#eq:norm-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-curvature">[eq:norm-curvature]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="subsec:norm-deduction"></a>
#### From scalar bounds to the norm estimate

<a id="eq:scalar-L"></a>
Define, for $z>0$, $$\label{eq:scalar-L}
 \mathcal L(z)=c\left(
 \frac{p\beta^2 zI(z)-2nG(z)^2}{wd_*}
 +\frac{D(z)^2}{1-w}\right).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-estimates"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-estimates" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:scalar-mean-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:scalar-pointwise"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *The constants and functions above satisfy $$\label{eq:scalar-mean-bound}
