 c\left(\frac{2n\beta^2}{wd_*}
       -\frac{(1-\beta)^2}{1-w}\right)
 \le\frac{2k-1}{k^2}$$ and $$\label{eq:scalar-pointwise}
 \mathcal L(z)\le2W(z)\qquad(z>0).$$ Equality in <a href="#eq:scalar-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-pointwise">[eq:scalar-pointwise]</a> holds only at $z=1$.*

</div>

We first deduce the norm estimate from the two scalar bounds. Their verification, including the strict pointwise inequality in every dimension, occupies the remainder of the section.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-estimates</summary>

<a id="eq:norm-mean-range"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:norm-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:norm-estimate">11</a>.* In the following expectations, $G,D,I$ denote their compositions with $g$. Set $$m=\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^k-1,\qquad \ell=\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1}-1,\qquad t=\mathbb E_{\sigma}G.$$ The normalization gives $\mathbb E_{\sigma}e(g)=0$, so $m=\mathbb E_{\sigma}M(g)\ge0$, $\mathbb E_{\sigma}D=m-t$, and $\mathbb E_{\sigma}W(g)=m-\ell$. Since $0\le G\le\beta M$, we have $0\le t\le\beta m$. For every $a>0$, Jensen’s inequality applied to the convex function $y\mapsto y^{-a/n}$ yields $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^a\ge(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{-n})^{-a/n}=1$. Together with $$z^{k-1}\le\frac{(k-1)z^k+1}{k},$$ this gives $$\label{eq:norm-mean-range}
 0\le\ell\le\frac{k-1}{k}\,m.$$ For $k=1$ this simply says $\ell=0$.

<a id="eq:norm-reduction"></a>
Since $Q$ annihilates constants, $Q(g^k)=QG+QD$. The Hilbert-space inequality $$\|a+b\|^2\le\frac{\|a\|^2}{w}
                       +\frac{\|b\|^2}{1-w}
 \qquad(0<w<1)$$ and $\|QD\|_2^2\le\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(D)$, followed by Lemma <a href="#lem:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:curvature">12</a>, give $$\begin{aligned}
 c\|Q(g^k)\|_2^2
 &\le\frac{c}{wd_*}
       \left(p\beta^2\mathbb E_{\sigma}[gI(g)]-2n\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(G)\right)
       +\frac{c}{1-w}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(D)\notag\\
 &=\mathbb E_{\sigma}\mathcal L(g)+c\left(
       \frac{2n}{wd_*}t^2-\frac{(m-t)^2}{1-w}\right).
 \label{eq:norm-reduction}
\end{aligned}$$ The quadratic expression in parentheses is increasing for $0\le t\le m$: its derivative is $$\frac{4n}{wd_*}t+\frac{2(m-t)}{1-w}\ge0.$$ It is therefore no larger than its value at $t=\beta m$. Using <a href="#eq:scalar-mean-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-mean-bound">[eq:scalar-mean-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:norm-mean-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-mean-range">[eq:norm-mean-range]</a>, we conclude that the final term of <a href="#eq:norm-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-reduction">[eq:norm-reduction]</a> is at most $$\frac{2k-1}{k^2}m^2\le m^2-\ell^2.$$ Now <a href="#eq:scalar-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-pointwise">[eq:scalar-pointwise]</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 c\|Q(g^k)\|_2^2
 &\le 2\mathbb E_{\sigma}W(g)+m^2-\ell^2\\
 &=2(m-\ell)+m^2-\ell^2
   =(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^k)^2-(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1})^2,
\end{aligned}$$ as claimed. If $g$ is not identically $1$ on the sphere, continuity gives a set of positive spherical measure on which $g\ne1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-estimates">13</a> then gives $\mathbb E_{\sigma}\mathcal L(g)<2\mathbb E_{\sigma}W(g)$, so the final inequality is strict. The only constant norm on the sphere with $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{-n}=1$ is $g=1$; in this case both sides of <a href="#eq:norm-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-estimate">[eq:norm-estimate]</a> vanish. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Proof of the scalar bounds

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-estimates-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-estimates</summary>

<a id="eq:scalar-c"></a>
*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-estimates">13</a>.* We first record the values of the spectral constant: $$\label{eq:scalar-c}
 c=\frac{121}{35}\quad(n=4),\qquad
 c=\frac{n^2+5n+7}{(n+1)(n+3)}\quad(n\ge5).$$ For $n=4,5,6$ the left side of <a href="#eq:scalar-mean-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-mean-bound">[eq:scalar-mean-bound]</a> is, respectively, $$\frac{11}{12},\qquad \frac{589}{864},\qquad \frac{1387}{1890}.$$ These are smaller than $1,3/4,3/4$, the respective right sides. For $n\ge7$ the left side is $$\frac{2(3n-4)(n^2+5n+7)}{9(n+1)(n+3)(n+4)}.$$ Its difference from $3/4$, with the latter first, is $$\frac{3n^3+128n^2+505n+548}{36(n+1)(n+3)(n+4)}>0.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:scalar-mean-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-mean-bound">[eq:scalar-mean-bound]</a> for every dimension.

<a id="eq:scalar-local-margin"></a>
*The interval $0<z\le1$.* Here $G=I=0$ and $|D(z)|=|z^k-1|\le k|z-1|$. Also $W(1)=W'(1)=0$ and $$W''(z)=\begin{cases}
 (n+1)z^{-n-2},&k=1,\\
 2+(n+1)z^{-n-2},&k=2.
 \end{cases}$$ Thus $2W(z)\ge(n+2k-1)(z-1)^2$. The positive margins $$\label{eq:scalar-local-margin}
 n+2k-1-\frac{ck^2}{1-w}
 =\begin{cases}
 3/5,&n=4,\\
 7/8,&n=5,\\
 43/21,&n=6,\\
 n+3-8c,&n\ge7
 \end{cases}$$ give the desired strict inequality for $z<1$. In the last case the margin is $9/10$ at $n=7$ and increases thereafter: regarding $n$ as a real variable in <a href="#eq:scalar-c" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-c">[eq:scalar-c]</a>, $$c'(n)=-\frac{n^2+8n+13}{(n+1)^2(n+3)^2}<0.$$ At $z=1$ both sides of <a href="#eq:scalar-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-pointwise">[eq:scalar-pointwise]</a> vanish.

<a id="eq:small-polynomials"></a>
*The interval $z>1$ in dimensions $4,5,6$.* Set $x=z-1>0$. Multiplication by a positive factor clears the negative powers of $z$: if $$P_n(z)=s_nz^{2n}\bigl(2W(z)-\mathcal L(z)\bigr),
 \qquad (s_4,s_5,s_6)=(2880,712800,221130),$$ then substitution of <a href="#eq:scalar-basic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-basic">[eq:scalar-basic]</a>–<a href="#eq:scalar-L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-L">[eq:scalar-L]</a> and collection of equal powers give $$\begin{aligned}
 P_4(z)={}&462z^{10}-983z^9+93z^8
             +3399z^5-3378z^4+407,\notag\\
 P_5(z)={}&151525z^{14}-273570z^{12}-680800z^{11}
             +938157z^{10}\notag\\
         &\quad+225720z^7-462264z^5+101232,\notag\\
 P_6(z)={}&48399z^{16}-75894z^{14}-218004z^{13}
             +285961z^{12}\notag\\
         &\quad+56940z^8-127478z^6+30076.
 \label{eq:small-polynomials}
\end{aligned}$$ For clarity, the coefficients needed from $P_n(1+x)$ are listed below; the constant and linear coefficients are zero. $$\begin{array}{c|rrr}
 [x^j]&n=4&n=5&n=6\\ \hline
 j=2&1728&623700&452790\\
 j=3&-1446&-1505900&679380\\
 j=4&-6711&-5802775&-132249\\
 j=5&1173&12849430&7800780\\
 j=6&17052&86314305&51269452\\
 j=7&20796&191506920&146058120
\end{array}$$ All remaining coefficients are nonnegative, as can be seen without listing them. Let $a_nz^{d_n}$ be the leading term in <a href="#eq:small-polynomials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-polynomials">[eq:small-polynomials]</a>. At $j=8$, its contribution $a_n\binom{d_n}{8}$ minus the contributions of all negative terms is $$11943,\qquad 207280425,\qquad 114414300
 \quad\text{for } n=4,5,6,$$ respectively. For every $i<d_n$, the ratio $\binom{i}{j}/\binom{d_n}{j}$ is nonincreasing in $j$; while it is nonzero, its ratio at successive indices is $(i-j)/(d_n-j)\le1$. Once $j>i$ it is zero. Thus the same leading term covers all negative contributions for every $8\le j\le d_n$.

It follows that $P_n(1+x)/x^2$ is bounded below by, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 U_4(x)&=1728-1446x-6711x^2+17052x^4,\\
 U_5(x)&=623700-1505900x-5802775x^2+86314305x^4,\\
 U_6(x)&=452790-132249x^2+51269452x^4.
\end{aligned}$$ These lower bounds are positive. Indeed $$\begin{aligned}
 1446x&\le400+1400x^2,&
 8111x^2&\le1000+17052x^4,\\
 1505900x&\le200000+2900000x^2,&
 8702775x^2&\le230000+86314305x^4.
\end{aligned}$$ Each follows from $dt\le a+bt^2$ when $d^2\le4ab$; the corresponding values of $4ab-d^2$ are $$149084,\quad 2419679,\quad 52265190000,\quad 3670867899375.$$ The first row gives $U_4(x)\ge328$ and the second gives $U_5(x)\ge193700$. Finally $U_6$, regarded as a quadratic in $x^2$, has positive leading coefficient and negative discriminant, since $$4\cdot452790\cdot51269452-132249^2=92839690886319>0.$$ Therefore $P_n(z)>0$ for $z>1$ in all three dimensions.

<a id="eq:large-polynomial"></a>
<a id="eq:large-coefficients"></a>
*The interval $z>1$ in all dimensions $n\ge7$.* Put $H=18(n+4)/c$. Direct collection of powers in <a href="#eq:scalar-L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-L">[eq:scalar-L]</a> now gives $$\label{eq:large-polynomial}
 \frac{9(n+4)}{c}z^{2n}(2W-\mathcal L)
 =z^{2n}(A_4z^4+A_2z^2+A_1z+A_0)
   +z^n(B_2z^2+B_0)+T_0,$$ where $$\begin{aligned}
 A_4&=\frac{2n-8}{3},&
 A_2&=H-12n-32-\frac{64}{n},\notag\\
 A_1&=-H+\frac{16(n-1)}3+32-\frac{16(n-1)}{2n+1},&&\notag\\
 A_0&=-\frac Hn+6n+40+\frac{64}{n}-\frac{128}{n^2},&&\notag\\
 B_2&=16+\frac{64}{n},&
 B_0&=\frac Hn-48-\frac{64}{n}+\frac{256}{n^2},\notag\\
 T_0&=\frac{16(n-1)}{2n+1}-\frac{128}{n^2}.&&
 \label{eq:large-coefficients}
\end{aligned}$$ We prove that the polynomial on the right of <a href="#eq:large-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-polynomial">[eq:large-polynomial]</a>, expressed in $x=z-1$, has nonnegative coefficients and a positive quadratic coefficient.

<a id="eq:large-quadratic"></a>
Write its two parentheses as $\sum_{i=0}^4q_ix^i$ and $\sum_{i=0}^2r_ix^i$. Thus $$\begin{aligned}
 q_0&=A_4+A_2+A_1+A_0,&q_1&=4A_4+2A_2+A_1,\\
 q_2&=6A_4+A_2,&q_3&=4A_4,\qquad q_4=A_4,\\
 r_0&=B_2+B_0,&r_1&=2B_2,\qquad r_2=B_2.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular $q_3,q_4,r_1,r_2>0$. The constant and linear coefficients of the full polynomial vanish. Its quadratic coefficient is $$\label{eq:large-quadratic}
 \frac{9(n+4)}c\,(n+3-8c)>0.$$ One can obtain these three coefficients either from <a href="#eq:large-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-coefficients">[eq:large-coefficients]</a> or directly from the order of vanishing at $z=1$: $G=O(x^2)$, $I=O(x^3)$, and $D=2x+O(x^2)$, so that $2W-\mathcal L=(n+3-8c)x^2+O(x^3)$. Positivity is <a href="#eq:scalar-local-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-local-margin">[eq:scalar-local-margin]</a>.

The remaining signs admit explicit polynomial certificates. Set $$\begin{aligned}
 V_0(n)&=34n^5-95n^4-1666n^3-5701n^2-8052n-2688,\\
 V_1(n)&=2n^5+9n^4-162n^3-845n^2-1220n-448,\\
 V_2(n)&=5n^3+8n^2-41n-56,\\
 R(n)&=7n^4+8n^3-187n^2-748n-896.
\end{aligned}$$ Substituting $H=18(n+4)(n+1)(n+3)/(n^2+5n+7)$ gives <a id="eq:large-signs"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 q_0+\frac{r_0}{8}
 &=\frac{V_0(n)}{4n^2(2n+1)(n^2+5n+7)},\notag\\
 q_1&=\frac{2V_1(n)}{n(2n+1)(n^2+5n+7)},\notag\\
 q_2&=\frac{2(n+4)V_2(n)}{n(n^2+5n+7)},&
 r_0&=-\frac{2R(n)}{n^2(n^2+5n+7)}.
 \label{eq:large-signs}
\end{aligned}$$ The required signs hold throughout their stated ranges because, for $t\ge0$, <a id="eq:shift-certificates"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 V_0(10+t)={}&34t^5+1605t^4+28534t^3
                  +227319t^2\notag\\
             &\quad+698128t+130692,\notag\\
 V_1(10+t)={}&2t^5+109t^4+2198t^3+19695t^2
                  +69280t+30852,\notag\\
 V_2(7+t)={}&5t^3+113t^2+806t+1764,\notag\\
 R(7+t)={}&7t^4+204t^3+2039t^2+7414t+4256.
 \label{eq:shift-certificates}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently $q_2>0$ and $r_0<0$ for every $n\ge7$, while $q_0+r_0/8>0$ and $q_1>0$ for every $n\ge10$.

<a id="eq:binomial-comparison"></a>
Use the convention that $\binom mj=0$ when $j<0$ or $j>m$. For $3\le j\le2n$, comparison of the factors in the binomial products gives $$\label{eq:binomial-comparison}
 \binom nj\le 2^{-j}\binom{2n}{j}.$$ Indeed $(n-a)/(2n-a)\le1/2$ for $0\le a<n$; if $j>n$ the left side is zero. For $n\ge10$, the sum of the $q_0$ and $r_0$ contributions to the coefficient of $x^j$ is therefore at least $$\left(q_0+\frac{r_0}{2^j}\right)\binom{2n}{j}
 \ge\left(q_0+\frac{r_0}{8}\right)\binom{2n}{j}>0.$$ All other contributions are nonnegative. For $j>2n$, the $q_0$ and $r_0$ contributions vanish and every remaining term is nonnegative. Together with <a href="#eq:large-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-quadratic">[eq:large-quadratic]</a>, this proves coefficient positivity for every $n\ge10$.

<a id="eq:small-large-dimension-bound"></a>
It remains to treat $n=7,8,9$, where $q_1$ can be negative. For $3\le j\le2n$, divide the coefficient of $x^j$ by $\binom{2n}{j}$, discard the nonnegative $q_3,q_4,r_2$ contributions, and use <a href="#eq:binomial-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binomial-comparison">[eq:binomial-comparison]</a> for the negative $r_0$ term. The resulting lower bound is $$\label{eq:small-large-dimension-bound}
 \begin{split}
 q_0+\frac{r_0}{2^j}
 +\frac{j}{2n-j+1}
   \left(q_1+q_2\frac{j-1}{2n-j+2}\right)
 +r_1\frac{\binom n{j-1}}{\binom{2n}{j}}.
 \end{split}$$ Substitution in <a href="#eq:large-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-coefficients">[eq:large-coefficients]</a> gives the componentwise lower bounds $$\begin{array}{c|rrrrr}
 n&q_0&q_1&q_2&r_0&r_1\\ \hline
 7&-2&-11&60&-2&50\\
 8&-1&-6&72&-6&46\\
 9&-1&-6&72&-6&46
\end{array}$$ All weights in <a href="#eq:small-large-dimension-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-large-dimension-bound">[eq:small-large-dimension-bound]</a> are nonnegative, so these bounds may be substituted directly. They give the following positive numbers at $j=3,4$; at $j=5$ we have already omitted the final $r_1$ term: $$\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 n&j=3&j=4&j=5\ \text{without the final term}\\ \hline
 7&5/26&1233/1144&589/176\\
 8&37/28&10707/3640&1153/208\\
 9&101/136&3851/2040&395/112
\end{array}$$ These finite substitutions can also be checked from the exact tuples $(q_0,q_1,q_2,r_0,r_1)$: $$\begin{aligned}
 n=7:&\quad
 \left(-\frac{5984}{3185},-\frac{4832}{455},
       \frac{792}{13},-\frac{1216}{637},\frac{352}{7}\right),\\
 n=8:&\quad
 \left(-\frac{421}{629},-\frac{3736}{629},
       \frac{2688}{37},-\frac{145}{37},48\right),\\
 n=9:&\quad
 \left(\frac{2416}{10773},-\frac{2192}{1197},
       \frac{100568}{1197},-\frac{57968}{10773},\frac{416}{9}\right).
\end{aligned}$$ For $5\le j\le2n$, the parenthesis $q_1+q_2(j-1)/(2n-j+2)$ is positive and increasing in $j$. In fact the displayed componentwise bounds give at $j=5$ the positive lower bounds $119/11$, $210/13$, and $66/5$, respectively. The prefactor $j/(2n-j+1)$ is positive and increasing too, while $r_0/2^j$ increases because $r_0<0$. The expression <a href="#eq:small-large-dimension-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:small-large-dimension-bound">[eq:small-large-dimension-bound]</a> with its last term omitted is therefore increasing throughout this range. Positivity at $j=5$ proves positivity at every subsequent index through $2n$.

For $j>2n$ all $r_i$ contributions vanish, since $n+2<2n+1$. The only possibly negative term is the $q_1$ contribution at $j=2n+1$. At this index the $q_1$ and $q_2$ contributions sum to $q_1+2nq_2$, which the same componentwise bounds make at least $829,1146,1290$ for $n=7,8,9$, respectively. For $j\ge2n+2$ only the nonnegative $q_2,q_3,q_4$ terms remain. This handles every coefficient, including the endpoints.

We have proved that the polynomial in <a href="#eq:large-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-polynomial">[eq:large-polynomial]</a> has nonnegative coefficients and a strictly positive coefficient of $x^2$ for every $n\ge7$. It is consequently positive for every $x>0$. Its multiplier $9(n+4)z^{2n}/c$ is positive, so $2W(z)-\mathcal L(z)>0$ for $z>1$. This finishes the proof of <a href="#eq:scalar-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-pointwise">[eq:scalar-pointwise]</a> and its strictness in every dimension. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:position"></a>
### Variational representation and affine position

We next represent a support functional by a vector field of the form <a href="#eq:vector-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vector-field">[eq:vector-field]</a>, and choose coordinates in which the associated norm power has no degree-two component. Throughout this section, $k$ has the value in <a href="#eq:k-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:k-choice">[eq:k-choice]</a>, and $\eta$ is a finite positive Borel measure with bounded support spanning $\mathbb R^n$. Neither symmetry of $\eta$ nor the functional condition <a href="#eq:functional-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-condition">[eq:functional-condition]</a> is needed here.

<a id="eq:variational-objective"></a>
Let $A$ be a nonzero symmetric positive semidefinite matrix, and write $V=\mathop{\mathrm{range}}(A)$. For an origin-symmetric convex body $L$ in $V$, with interior relative to $V$, put $$\tau_A(L)=\int h_L(Ax)\,d\eta(x).$$ We extend its gauge to $\mathbb R^n$ by $g=g_L\circ P_V$, where $P_V$ denotes orthogonal projection onto $V$. This extension is a seminorm, with kernel $V^\perp$. Define $$\label{eq:variational-objective}
 J(g)=
 \begin{cases}
  \mathbb E_{\sigma}\log g,&k=1,\\
  \mathbb E_{\sigma}g,&k=2.
 \end{cases}$$ The logarithm is integrated outside the kernel, which is a spherical null set. When $A$ is positive definite, these objectives are chosen so that their Wulff first variations involve $g^{k-1}\nabla g=X_{g^k}$, the vector field in <a href="#eq:norm-vector-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-vector-field">[eq:norm-vector-field]</a>.

This variational construction belongs to the framework for dual curvature measures. The optimizer-to-measure argument corresponds to the $L_1$ dual Minkowski problem with dual parameter $q=1-k$: for $k=2$, compare <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[HuangZhao2018](#ref-HuangZhao2018), Section 3.1, Lemmas 3.1–3.3]; for $k=1$ and full-dimensional bodies, minimization under $\tau_A(L)=1$ is equivalent, by polarity, to the $p=1$ entropy maximization in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HuangLutwakYangZhang2018](#ref-HuangLutwakYangZhang2018), Section 5, Lemmas 5.1–5.3]. Wang and Zhou established uniqueness and continuity for the relevant full-dimensional dual Minkowski problems <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[WangZhou2026](#ref-WangZhou2026), arXiv version, Theorems 1.1–1.3]. Here we prove the representation in our normalization, together with uniqueness and continuity through changes of rank, where the gauges become seminorms. This continuity is required to select affine coordinates. The measures may have atoms, and all variations remain valid for nonsmooth minimizing bodies.

<a id="stmt-lem-minimizer"></a>
<div id="lem:minimizer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *For every nonzero symmetric positive semidefinite $A$, there is a unique origin-symmetric convex body $L_A$ in $V=\mathop{\mathrm{range}}(A)$ that minimizes <a href="#eq:variational-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variational-objective">[eq:variational-objective]</a> subject to $\tau_A(L_A)=1$. Denote its extended gauge by $g_A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-minimizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-minimizer</summary>

*Proof.* The spanning hypothesis gives a norm on $V$: $$q_A(y)=\int |y\cdot Ax|\,d\eta(x).$$ Indeed, $q_A(y)=0$ implies that $Ay$ is orthogonal to $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta$, and hence $Ay=0$; because $y\in V$, this forces $y=0$. For any normalized competitor $L$ and $y\in L$, symmetry gives $q_A(y)\le\tau_A(L)=1$. Thus all normalized competitors have a common outer radius in $V$.

<a id="eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound"></a>
We also need a bound on their gauges along a minimizing sequence. For any nonzero seminorm $g$, let $R=\max_{S^{n-1}}g$. By separation, $g$ is the support function of the compact symmetric set $\{z:z\cdot y\le g(y)\text{ for every }y\in\mathbb R^n\}$. This set has maximal radius $R$: if $Re$ is a point of maximal norm in it, then $$\label{eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound}
 g(u)\ge R|u\cdot e|\qquad(u\in S^{n-1}).$$ Consequently, $$J(g)\ge
 \begin{cases}
  \log R+\mathbb E_{\sigma}\log|u_1|,&k=1,\\
  Rb_n,&k=2.
 \end{cases}$$ Here $\mathbb E_{\sigma}|\log|u_1||^2<\infty$: the first coordinate has a density proportional to $(1-t^2)^{(n-3)/2}$ on $(-1,1)$, and $\int_0^{1/2}|\log t|^2\,dt<\infty$. In particular every nonzero seminorm has a finite logarithmic objective.

Set $b_A=\int|Ax|\,d\eta(x)>0$. The ball of radius $1/b_A$ in $V$ is a normalized competitor. Comparison with this ball bounds $J$ above along a minimizing sequence, so the preceding inequalities bound $R$ above. The bodies therefore contain a common inner ball in $V$, as well as lying in a common outer ball. Their gauges are uniformly bounded and equicontinuous on the unit sphere of $V$. A subsequence converges uniformly to the gauge of a body with the same two ball bounds. The support functions converge uniformly as well, so the normalization passes to the limit. The objective also passes to the limit: in the logarithmic case the extended gauges are uniformly comparable to $|P_Vu|$, whose logarithm is integrable by <a href="#eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound">[eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound]</a>. This proves existence.

For uniqueness, let $L_0,L_1$ be two minimizers and $L_t=(1-t)L_0+tL_1$, where $0<t<1$. Its normalization remains one. For $P_Vu\ne0$, write $\rho_i(u)=1/g_{L_i}(P_Vu)$. Taking points on the ray through $P_Vu$ gives $$\frac{1}{g_{L_t}(P_Vu)}
 \ge (1-t)\rho_0(u)+t\rho_1(u).$$ Both $-\log\rho$ and $1/\rho$ are strictly convex decreasing functions of $\rho>0$. Thus the objective of $L_t$ is at most the interpolated objectives, and the inequality is strict wherever $\rho_0\ne\rho_1$. Distinct bodies have different radial functions on an open set of directions in $V$, which gives a set of positive spherical measure after projection. They would therefore give a strictly smaller objective, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-representation"></a>
<div id="prop:representation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:representation"></a>
<a id="eq:norm-power-field"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Variational representation). *Suppose $A$ is positive definite, and let $L$ be any positive dilate of $L_A$, with gauge $g=g_L$. For every continuous even function $\phi:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ that is positively homogeneous of degree one, $$\label{eq:representation}
 \int\phi(Ax)\,d\eta(x)
 =s\,\mathbb E_{\sigma}\phi\bigl(X_{g^k}(u)\bigr),
 \qquad
 s=\frac{\tau_A(L)}{\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1}}.$$ Here, almost everywhere on the sphere, $$\label{eq:norm-power-field}
 X_{g^k}=g^ku+g^{k-1}\nabla_Sg=g^{k-1}\nabla g.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-representation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-representation</summary>

<a id="eq:wulff-minimum"></a>
*Proof.* Normalization turns the minimization problem into minimizing $J(\tau_A(L)g_L)$ over all symmetric bodies. Put $\tau=\tau_A(L)$ and $I=\int\phi(Ax)\,d\eta(x)$. For small positive or negative $t$, define the body $$W_t=\bigl\{y\in\mathbb R^n:
       y\cdot v\le h_L(v)+t\phi(v)\text{ for all }v\in S^{n-1}\bigr\},
 \qquad g_t=g_{W_t}.$$ The constraint is positive for sufficiently small $|t|$, so $W_t$ is a symmetric convex body. Although that constraint need not be a support function, it bounds the actual support function above. Therefore $$\tau_A(W_t)\le\tau+tI.$$ Both quantities are positive for small $|t|$. Since $J$ increases under pointwise increase of a positive gauge, minimality gives $$\label{eq:wulff-minimum}
 J\bigl((\tau+tI)g_t\bigr)
 \ge J\bigl(\tau_A(W_t)g_t\bigr)
 \ge J(\tau g),$$ with equality at $t=0$.

Lemma <a href="#lem:gauge-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gauge-variation">2</a> gives $$\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}g_t(u)
 =-g(u)\phi(\nabla g(u))$$ almost everywhere, with uniform bounds that permit differentiation of the objective. The left side of <a href="#eq:wulff-minimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wulff-minimum">[eq:wulff-minimum]</a> has a two-sided minimum at zero. Its derivative is consequently zero: $$\frac{I}{\tau}=\mathbb E_{\sigma}\phi(\nabla g)\quad(k=1),
 \qquad
 I\mathbb E_{\sigma}g=\tau\mathbb E_{\sigma}\bigl[g\phi(\nabla g)\bigr]\quad(k=2).$$ Equivalently, $$\frac{I}{\tau}
 =\frac{\mathbb E_{\sigma}\bigl[g^{k-1}\phi(\nabla g)\bigr]}{\mathbb E_{\sigma}g^{k-1}}.$$ Euler’s identity for the homogeneous gauge gives <a href="#eq:norm-power-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:norm-power-field">[eq:norm-power-field]</a>, and positive homogeneity of $\phi$ then gives <a href="#eq:representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:representation">[eq:representation]</a>. The argument applies to both signs of $t$ without imposing convexity on the varied constraint. ◻

</details>

To select a position we allow the matrix to approach the boundary of the compact convex set <a id="eq:matrix-domain"></a>$$\label{eq:matrix-domain}
 \mathcal A=\{A=A^t\ge0:\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A=1\}.$$ The minimizers extend continuously to this boundary, even though their ambient unit sets may become cylinders.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-continuity"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-continuity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *The map $A\mapsto g_A|_{S^{n-1}}$ is continuous on $\mathcal A$ in the uniform norm.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-continuity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-continuity</summary>

*Proof.* Let $A_j\to A$ in $\mathcal A$, and write $V_j=\mathop{\mathrm{range}}(A_j)$, $g_j=g_{A_j}$, and $b_j=\int|A_jx|\,d\eta(x)$. Then $b_j\to b=\int|Ax|\,d\eta(x)>0$. The normalized ball in $V_j$ has extended gauge $b_j|P_{V_j}u|$, and hence objective at most $\log b_j$ if $k=1$, and at most $b_j$ if $k=2$. By <a href="#eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound">[eq:seminorm-coordinate-bound]</a>, this bounds $R_j=\max_{S^{n-1}}g_j$ uniformly above. The minimizing body contains the ball of radius $1/R_j$ in $V_j$, so its normalization gives $1\ge b_j/R_j$. Thus $R_j\ge b_j$, bounded away from zero.

<a id="eq:objective-limit"></a>
The seminorms $g_j$ have uniformly bounded Lipschitz constants. Every subsequence therefore has a further subsequence converging uniformly on $S^{n-1}$ to a nonzero seminorm $g$. Along such a subsequence, $$\label{eq:objective-limit}
 J(g_j)\longrightarrow J(g).$$ For $k=2$ this is immediate. For $k=1$, choose unit vectors $e_j$ with $g_j(u)\ge R_j|u\cdot e_j|$. The positive parts of $\log g_j$ are uniformly bounded, and their negative parts have uniformly bounded squared integrals, by rotation invariance and $\mathbb E_{\sigma}|\log|u_1||^2<\infty$. These logarithms are therefore uniformly integrable. They converge almost everywhere to $\log g$, since the kernel of a nonzero seminorm is a proper subspace. Truncating the logarithms below at $-M$ and then sending $M$ to infinity proves <a href="#eq:objective-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:objective-limit">[eq:objective-limit]</a>; the discarded integrals are uniformly $O(M^{-1})$ by the squared-integral bound.

<a id="eq:cylinder-normalization"></a>
We identify this subsequential limit. Set $Z=\{g\le1\}$ and $Z_j=\{g_j\le1\}$, allowing infinite values for their support functions. If $g(y)<1$, then $y\in Z_j$ for all sufficiently large $j$. Testing the support functions against $y$ and then taking the supremum gives, for every $x$, <a id="eq:cylinder-support-limit"></a>$$\label{eq:cylinder-support-limit}
 h_Z(Ax)\le\liminf_j h_{Z_j}(A_jx).$$ The supremum over $g(y)<1$ equals $h_Z$, by scaling towards zero. Since $Z_j=L_{A_j}+V_j^\perp$ and $A_jx\in V_j$, its support function at $A_jx$ equals that of $L_{A_j}$. All these support functions are nonnegative, so Fatou’s lemma yields $$\label{eq:cylinder-normalization}
 \int h_Z(Ax)\,d\eta(x)\le1.$$

Let $V=\mathop{\mathrm{range}}(A)$ and $L_0=\overline{P_VZ}$. This projection is bounded despite the possible unboundedness of $Z$. Indeed, for every $z\in Z$, symmetry and <a href="#eq:cylinder-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cylinder-normalization">[eq:cylinder-normalization]</a> give $$q_A(P_Vz)=\int|z\cdot Ax|\,d\eta(x)
 \le\int h_Z(Ax)\,d\eta(x)\le1.$$ The norm $q_A$ bounds the radius in $V$. Moreover $Z$ contains a Euclidean ball, so $L_0$ has interior in $V$. Its extended gauge $\bar g=g_{L_0}\circ P_V$ satisfies $\bar g\le g$, and <a id="eq:projected-normalization"></a>$$\label{eq:projected-normalization}
 0<c_0:=\tau_A(L_0)=\int h_Z(Ax)\,d\eta(x)\le1.$$

<a id="eq:full-body-comparison"></a>
For the opposite comparison, let $N$ be any full-dimensional symmetric convex body in $\mathbb R^n$. The extended gauge of its projection to $V_j$ is at most $g_N$, and this projected body’s normalization is $\tau_{A_j}(N)$. Minimality and monotonicity give $$J(g_j)\le J\bigl(\tau_{A_j}(N)g_N\bigr).$$ Passing to the limit, using <a href="#eq:objective-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:objective-limit">[eq:objective-limit]</a> and continuity of $\tau_{A_j}(N)$, gives $$\label{eq:full-body-comparison}
 J(g)\le J\bigl(\tau_A(N)g_N\bigr).$$ Take $N=N_R=L_A+RB_{V^\perp}$, the orthogonal product of the minimizing body in $V$ and a ball of radius $R$ in $V^\perp$. Then $\tau_A(N_R)=1$ and $$g_{N_R}(u)=\max\{g_A(u),|P_{V^\perp}u|/R\}\downarrow g_A(u).$$ For $k=1$, the integrable function $\log g_A$ is a lower bound for these logarithms, and their positive parts are uniformly bounded for $R\ge1$; hence the objectives converge. The case $k=2$ is immediate. Thus <a href="#eq:full-body-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-body-comparison">[eq:full-body-comparison]</a> implies $J(g)\le J(g_A)$.

Finally, $c_0\bar g$ is the extended gauge of the normalized body $L_0/c_0$. Since $c_0\bar g\le g$, we have $$J(g_A)\le J(c_0\bar g)\le J(g)\le J(g_A).$$ Strict increase of the objective integrands forces $c_0\bar g=g$ almost everywhere, and then everywhere by continuity. Uniqueness in Lemma <a href="#lem:minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:minimizer">14</a> gives $c_0\bar g=g_A$. Thus every subsequential limit is $g_A$, which proves the asserted continuity. ◻

</details>

We use Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem to complete the choice of position. We record the compact-convex formulation and its short reduction to the simplex theorem <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Brouwer1911](#ref-Brouwer1911), Satz 4, p. 115]; the nearest-point inequality in the reduction will also be used below.

<a id="stmt-lem-fixed-point"></a>
<div id="lem:fixed-point" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (A finite-dimensional fixed-point principle). *Every continuous self-map of a nonempty compact convex subset of a finite-dimensional Euclidean space has a fixed point.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fixed-point"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fixed-point</summary>

*Proof.* Let the convex set be $D$, and work in its affine hull, of dimension $d$. The case $d=0$ is immediate. The nearest-point projection $\pi$ onto $D$ exists by compactness and is unique by strict convexity of squared distance along a segment between distinct minimizers. Minimizing along segments gives $$\langle x-\pi(x),z-\pi(x)\rangle\le0\qquad(z\in D).$$ Applying this twice yields $$|\pi(x)-\pi(y)|^2
 \le\langle x-y,\pi(x)-\pi(y)\rangle,$$ so $\pi$ is continuous. If $T:D\to D$ is continuous, choose a $d$-simplex $\Delta$ containing $D$ and set $G=T\circ\pi:\Delta\to\Delta$.

Brouwer’s theorem for a simplex gives $v=G(v)$. Since $G(\Delta)\subset D$, this point lies in $D$, hence $\pi(v)=v$ and $T(v)=v$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-position"></a>
<div id="prop:position" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (Affine position). *There is a symmetric positive definite matrix $A$ with $\det A=1$ such that $g_A^k$ has zero component in $\mathcal H_2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-position"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-position</summary>

*Proof.* On $\mathcal A$ define the symmetric traceless matrix <a id="eq:position-field"></a>$$\label{eq:position-field}
 \mathcal F(A)
 =\frac{\mathbb E_{\sigma}[g_A(u)^k uu^t]}{\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_A^k}-\frac1nI_n.$$ It is continuous by Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-continuity">16</a>; its denominator is positive because $g_A$ is a nonzero seminorm.

<a id="eq:kernel-field"></a>
The direction $-\mathcal F(A)$ points strictly into the positive cone along every missing direction of a singular matrix. To compute this, let $A$ be singular and $e$ a unit vector in $\mathop{\mathrm{ker}}A$. For a standard Gaussian $Y$ in $\mathbb R^n$, $g_A(Y)$ depends only on the projection of $Y$ to $\mathop{\mathrm{range}}(A)$, and is independent of $Y\cdot e$. Separating the Gaussian radial and angular variables therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\mathbb E_{\sigma}[g_A(u)^k(u\cdot e)^2]}{\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_A^k}
 &=\frac{\mathbb E[g_A(Y)^k(Y\cdot e)^2]}
          {\mathbb E g_A(Y)^k}
   \frac{\mathbb E|Y|^k}{\mathbb E|Y|^{k+2}}\\
 &=\frac{1}{n+k}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last radial moment ratio follows by integration by parts in $\int_0^\infty r^{n+k-1}e^{-r^2/2}\,dr$. It follows, by polarization on $\mathop{\mathrm{ker}}A$, that $$\label{eq:kernel-field}
 \left.\mathcal F(A)\right|_{\mathop{\mathrm{ker}}A}
 =-\frac{k}{n(n+k)}I_{\mathop{\mathrm{ker}}A}.$$ The range–kernel block of $\mathcal F(A)$ is zero: reflecting all kernel coordinates preserves $g_A$ and reverses each mixed product. Consequently $A-\epsilon\mathcal F(A)$ is positive definite for sufficiently small $\epsilon>0$. On the kernel this follows from <a href="#eq:kernel-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-field">[eq:kernel-field]</a>; on the range it follows from the positive definiteness of the restriction of $A$. Its trace is one. The same perturbation is allowed at a positive definite $A$ for sufficiently small $\epsilon$.

Use the Euclidean inner product $\langle U,W\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(UW)$ on symmetric matrices, and consider the continuous map $$A\longmapsto
 \operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal A}\bigl(A-\mathcal F(A)\bigr).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-point">17</a> provides a fixed point $A$. The nearest-point condition there is $$\langle\mathcal F(A),B-A\rangle\ge0
 \qquad(B\in\mathcal A).$$ We may take $B=A-\epsilon\mathcal F(A)$, as just shown. This gives $-\epsilon\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathcal F(A)^2)\ge0$, hence $\mathcal F(A)=0$. Formula <a href="#eq:kernel-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-field">[eq:kernel-field]</a> rules out singularity, so $A$ is positive definite.

For every symmetric traceless matrix $H$, the vanishing field gives $$\mathbb E_{\sigma}\bigl[g_A(u)^k u^tHu\bigr]=0.$$ These quadratics are exactly $\mathcal H_2$, since the Euclidean Laplacian of $x^tHx$ is $2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}H$. Thus the required degree-two component vanishes.

Finally, for $a>0$ the correspondence $L\mapsto L/a$ between normalized competitors for $A$ and $aA$ gives <a id="eq:minimizer-scaling"></a>$$\label{eq:minimizer-scaling}
 g_{aA}=a g_A.$$ Indeed, $\tau_{aA}(L/a)=\tau_A(L)$, while multiplying a gauge by $a$ adds $\log a$ to the logarithmic objective and multiplies the linear objective by $a$. The minimizers therefore correspond. Taking $a=(\det A)^{-1/n}$ makes the determinant one and preserves the vanishing degree-two component. ◻

</details>

Only one measure needs the position of Proposition <a href="#prop:position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:position">18</a>. For a second measure, Proposition <a href="#prop:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:representation">15</a> provides the required vector field in the resulting coordinates without a simultaneous position condition.

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### The bilinear inequality and equality cases

We now combine the spherical and variational estimates. Recall that an admissible measure satisfies the support-functional inequality <a href="#eq:functional-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-condition">[eq:functional-condition]</a>, and need not itself be symmetric.

<a id="stmt-thm-bilinear"></a>
<div id="thm:bilinear" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:bilinear"></a>
**Theorem 19**. *Let $\eta,\zeta$ be finite positive measures on $\mathbb R^n$ with bounded, spanning support. Suppose that both satisfy <a href="#eq:functional-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-condition">[eq:functional-condition]</a>. Then $$\label{eq:bilinear}
 \iint |x\cdot y|\,d\eta(x)d\zeta(y)\ge b_n.$$ If equality holds, there is an origin-centered ellipsoid $E_0$ such that $$\int h_{E_0}\,d\eta=(|E_0|/\kappa_n)^{1/n}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-bilinear"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-bilinear</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $k$ as in <a href="#eq:k-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:k-choice">[eq:k-choice]</a> and write $c=c_{n,k}$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:position">18</a> to $\eta$, obtaining a symmetric positive definite matrix $A$ with determinant one. Replace $\eta$ by its image under $A$ and $\zeta$ by its image under $A^{-1}$. Their pairing is unchanged, since $(Ax)\cdot(A^{-1}y)=x\cdot y$. Their support-functional inequalities also persist: for any linear map $T$, $$h_M(Tx)=h_{T^tM}(x),$$ and $|T^tM|=|M|$ when $|\det T|=1$.

<a id="eq:normalized-parameters"></a>
For the first transformed measure, the support functional at the identity is precisely the original $\tau_A$. Its unique minimizing body is therefore $L_A$, with the degree-two cancellation supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:position">18</a>. For each transformed measure choose its minimizing body as in Proposition <a href="#prop:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:representation">15</a>, and rescale that body to volume $\kappa_n$. Let the resulting gauges be $g_1,g_2$, and put $$f_i=g_i^k,\qquad t_i=\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i^{k-1},\qquad
 s_i=\frac{\tau_i(L_i)}{t_i},\qquad
 a_i=s_i\mathbb E_{\sigma}f_i\quad(i=1,2),$$ where $\tau_i$ is the functional of the corresponding transformed measure. Thus $$\label{eq:normalized-parameters}
 \mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i^{-n}=1,\qquad s_it_i\ge1,\qquad a_i\ge1.$$ For the last inequality, if $k=1$ then $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i\ge1=t_i$ by the negative-moment normalization. If $k=2$, the same normalization gives $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i\ge1$, and $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i^2\ge(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i)^2\ge\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_i=t_i$. The first function $f_1$ has no degree-two harmonic component; scalar rescaling does not alter this property.

<a id="eq:error-absorption"></a>
Apply <a href="#eq:representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:representation">[eq:representation]</a> to $|x\cdot y|$ successively in both variables. These are even continuous homogeneous tests, so the formula applies despite any asymmetry of the measures. It gives <a id="eq:pairing-sign"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \iint |x\cdot y|\,d\eta(x)d\zeta(y)
 &=s_1s_2\iint
       |X_{f_1}(u)\cdot X_{f_2}(v)|\,d\sigma(u)d\sigma(v)
       \notag\\
 &\ge b_ns_1s_2\mathcal B(f_1,f_2).
 \label{eq:pairing-sign}
\end{aligned}$$ The pointwise inequality in the last line uses the test $\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}(u\cdot v)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:sign-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sign-estimate">10</a> now gives $$b_n^{-1}\iint |x\cdot y|\,d\eta(x)d\zeta(y)
 \ge a_1a_2-s_1s_2c\left\lVert Qf_1\right\rVert_2\left\lVert Qf_2\right\rVert_2.$$ By Theorem <a href="#thm:norm-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:norm-estimate">11</a> and <a href="#eq:normalized-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalized-parameters">[eq:normalized-parameters]</a>, $$\label{eq:error-absorption}
 s_i^2c\left\lVert Qf_i\right\rVert_2^2
 \le a_i^2-s_i^2t_i^2\le a_i^2-1.$$ Consequently the last lower bound is at least $$a_1a_2-\sqrt{(a_1^2-1)(a_2^2-1)}\ge1.$$ Indeed $a_1a_2-1\ge0$ and $$(a_1a_2-1)^2-(a_1^2-1)(a_2^2-1)=(a_1-a_2)^2\ge0.$$ This proves <a href="#eq:bilinear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bilinear">[eq:bilinear]</a>.

Suppose equality holds. If $a_i>1$, the inequality $s_i^2c\left\lVert Qf_i\right\rVert_2^2\le a_i^2-1$ is strict: this follows from Theorem <a href="#thm:norm-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:norm-estimate">11</a> when $g_i$ is nonconstant, and from its zero left side when $g_i$ is constant. If both $a_i$ exceed one, the product error in <a href="#eq:error-absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:error-absorption">[eq:error-absorption]</a> is therefore strictly smaller than the displayed square root. If exactly one exceeds one, the error product vanishes and $a_1a_2>1$. Both alternatives contradict equality, so $a_1=a_2=1$. In particular, $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1^k\le\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1^{k-1}$. For $k=1$, this forces $\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1=1$ and equality in the negative-moment power-mean bound; hence $g_1=1$. For $k=2$, the chain $$1\le\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1\le(\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1)^2\le\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1^2\le\mathbb E_{\sigma}g_1$$ again forces constancy and then $g_1=1$. Thus $s_1=1$, and the support-functional condition of the transformed first measure is sharp on $B_2^n$. Undoing the transformation makes the original condition sharp on $A^tB_2^n$, which is an origin-centered ellipsoid of volume $\kappa_n$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-bilinear-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-bilinear</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Translate $K$ so that the origin is in its interior, and first rescale it to satisfy $|K|=\kappa_n$. Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-measure">6</a> supplies the admissible measure $\eta_K$. Set $$r=\left(\frac{|\Pi K|}{\kappa_n}\right)^{1/n},
 \qquad D=\frac1r\Pi K.$$ The symmetric body $D$ has volume $\kappa_n$, so it too has an admissible measure $\eta_D$. Its own support-functional integral is one: the volume derivative under $D+tD=(1+t)D$ gives $$\int h_D\,d\eta_D
 =\frac1{n\kappa_n}\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0+}|(1+t)D|=1.$$ Using <a href="#eq:projection-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projection-measure">[eq:projection-measure]</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:bilinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bilinear">19</a>, $$1=\frac{n\kappa_n}{2r}
       \iint|x\cdot y|\,d\eta_K(x)d\eta_D(y)
   \ge\frac{n\kappa_nb_n}{2r}.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:ball-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ball-constant">[eq:ball-constant]</a> yields $r\ge\kappa_{n-1}$, or $|\Pi K|\ge\kappa_n\kappa_{n-1}^n$ under the current normalization. The projection body scales as $\Pi(aK)=a^{n-1}\Pi K$ for $a>0$. Undoing the volume normalization proves <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a>.

If equality holds, the pairing of $\eta_K$ and $\eta_D$ equals $b_n$. Theorem <a href="#thm:bilinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:bilinear">19</a> gives an ellipsoid on which the support-functional bound for $\eta_K$ is sharp. The equality assertion of Proposition <a href="#prop:projection-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:projection-measure">6</a> then makes $K$ a translate of a positive dilate of that ellipsoid.

For the converse, the quotient is invariant under translations and invertible linear maps. To verify the latter directly, the segment volume derivative gives, for every vector $y$ and every invertible $T$, $$\begin{aligned}
 2h_{\Pi(TK)}(y)
 &=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0+}|TK+t[-y,y]|\\
 &=|\det T|\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{0+}
              |K+t[-T^{-1}y,T^{-1}y]|\\
 &=2|\det T|h_{\Pi K}(T^{-1}y).
\end{aligned}$$ Hence $\Pi(TK)=|\det T|T^{-t}\Pi K$ and $|\Pi(TK)|=|\det T|^{n-1}|\Pi K|$, exactly matching the factor in $|TK|^{n-1}$. Finally, $\Pi B_2^n=\kappa_{n-1}B_2^n$ gives equality for the ball, and therefore for every ellipsoid. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Consequences

Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, together with the separate three-dimensional theorem of Chen, Feng, Li, Xi, and Xu <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026](#ref-ChenEtAl2026), Theorem 1.1], gives Petty’s projection-volume inequality and its ellipsoid equality case in every dimension $n\ge3$. We now apply this combined conclusion to lower-degree projection bodies, affine functional inequalities, and isoperimetry in normed spaces. The implications and equality assertions are specified separately in each setting.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Lower-degree projection bodies

For a convex body $L\subset\mathbb R^m$, use the standard intrinsic volumes $V_j(L)$, normalized by the Steiner formula $$\operatorname{vol}_m(L+tB_2^m)
 =\sum_{j=0}^m\kappa_{m-j}V_j(L)t^{m-j},
 \qquad t\ge0,\qquad \kappa_0=1.$$ This is the normalization in <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[OrtegaMorenoSchuster2021](#ref-OrtegaMorenoSchuster2021), author version, Section 2, p. 6]; on a hyperplane we use its induced Euclidean structure. For $1\le i\le n-1$, the degree-$i$ projection body $\Pi_iK$ is defined by $$h_{\Pi_iK}(u)=V_i(K|u^\perp),\qquad u\in S^{n-1},$$ where $K|u^\perp=\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}K$. In particular, $\Pi_{n-1}K=\Pi K$ because $V_{n-1}$ on $u^\perp$ is its $(n-1)$-dimensional volume.

<a id="stmt-cor-lutwak-petty"></a>
<div id="cor:lutwak-petty" class="corollary">

**Corollary 20** (Lutwak–Petty lower-degree inequalities). *Let $n\ge3$ and $1\le i\le n-1$ be integers, and let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex body with nonempty interior. Then <a id="eq:lutwak-petty"></a>$$\label{eq:lutwak-petty}
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{V_{i+1}(\Pi_iK)}{V_{i+1}(K)^i}
 &\ge
 \frac{V_{i+1}(\Pi_iB_2^n)}{V_{i+1}(B_2^n)^i}\\
 &=
 \frac{\left(\displaystyle\binom{n-1}{i}
              \frac{\kappa_{n-1}}{\kappa_{n-i-1}}\right)^{i+1}}
      {\left(\displaystyle\binom{n}{i+1}
              \frac{\kappa_n}{\kappa_{n-i-1}}\right)^{i-1}}.
\end{aligned}$$ The lower bound is sharp, since $K=B_2^n$ attains it.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-lutwak-petty"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-lutwak-petty</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and the separately proved three-dimensional result <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026](#ref-ChenEtAl2026), Theorem 1.1] establish Petty’s conjecture for every $n\ge3$. Lutwak’s conditional result <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Lutwak1990](#ref-Lutwak1990)], in the explicit formulation of Ortega-Moreno and Schuster <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[OrtegaMorenoSchuster2021](#ref-OrtegaMorenoSchuster2021), author version, Introduction, p. 2], states that Petty’s conjecture implies the Euclidean-ball lower bound for these ratios for every $1\le i\le n-1$. Applying that implication gives the inequality. For its explicit value, the Steiner normalization gives $$V_j(B_2^m)=\binom{m}{j}\frac{\kappa_m}{\kappa_{m-j}},
 \qquad
 \Pi_iB_2^n=V_i(B_2^{n-1})B_2^n.$$ The $(i+1)$-homogeneity of $V_{i+1}$ now gives the displayed ball ratio. Only the inequality conclusion of the conditional result is used. ◻

</details>

At $i=n-1$, the displayed constant is $\kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n}$, exactly the constant in Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a>. The case $i=1$ was already established by Lutwak, as recalled in <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[OrtegaMorenoSchuster2021](#ref-OrtegaMorenoSchuster2021), author version, Introduction, p. 2]. Thus the additional range furnished here is $2\le i\le n-2$ for $n\ge4$. No classification of equality cases is asserted for Corollary <a href="#cor:lutwak-petty" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:lutwak-petty">20</a>.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A sharp affine $L^1$ Sobolev consequence

For functions $f_1,\ldots,f_n\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n)$, define the mixed determinant-gradient integral $$\mathcal D_n(f_1,\ldots,f_n)
 =\int_{(\mathbb R^n)^n}
 \left|\det\bigl(\nabla f_1(x_1),\ldots,\nabla f_n(x_n)\bigr)\right|
 \,dx_1\cdots dx_n.$$ The determinant is the volume of the spanned parallelepiped, with no factor $1/n!$. Under $f_i(x)\mapsto f_i(Ax+b)$ with $A$ invertible, this integral and the product of the $L^{n/(n-1)}$ norms below both scale by $|\det A|^{1-n}$.

<a id="stmt-cor-affine-sobolev"></a>
<div id="cor:affine-sobolev" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:mixed-affine-sobolev"></a>
<a id="eq:affine-sobolev"></a>
**Corollary 21** (Mixed determinant-gradient and affine Sobolev inequalities). *Let $n\ge3$ be an integer, put $q=n/(n-1)$, and let $f_1,\ldots,f_n\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n)$ be nonnegative. Then $$\label{eq:mixed-affine-sobolev}
 \mathcal D_n(f_1,\ldots,f_n)
 \ge n!\,\kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n}
       \prod_{i=1}^n\left\lVert f_i\right\rVert_q.$$ In particular, every nonnegative $f\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^n)$ satisfies the affine $L^1$ Sobolev inequality $$\label{eq:affine-sobolev}
 \mathcal D_n(f,\ldots,f)^{1/n}
 \ge (n!)^{1/n}\kappa_{n-1}\kappa_n^{(2-n)/n}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_q.$$ Both constants are sharp in this class, through smooth approximation of ellipsoid indicators in $BV(\mathbb R^n)$; no equality assertion for nonzero $C_c^1$ functions is made.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-affine-sobolev"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-affine-sobolev</summary>

*Proof.* Write $c_n=\kappa_{n-1}^{\,n}\kappa_n^{\,2-n}$. Haddad’s geometric functional $\widetilde I_1(K_1,\ldots,K_n)$ integrates the absolute determinant against the surface-area measures of the convex bodies $K_i$. He uses $$h_{\Pi K}(u)=\frac12\int_{S^{n-1}}|\left\langle u,v\right\rangle|\,dS_K(v),$$ which is the projection-volume normalization above by Cauchy’s projection formula. His mixed-volume identity and the Aleksandrov–Fenchel inequality give $$\frac1{n!}\widetilde I_1(K_1,\ldots,K_n)
 =V(\Pi K_1,\ldots,\Pi K_n)
 \ge\prod_{i=1}^n|\Pi K_i|^{1/n}.$$ Here $V$ denotes mixed volume, and Haddad’s $\omega_m$ is our unit-ball volume $\kappa_m$; see <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Haddad2020](#ref-Haddad2020), arXiv version, Introduction, p. 5]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> for $n\ge4$ and the separately proved three-dimensional case <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026](#ref-ChenEtAl2026), Theorem 1.1] therefore give $$\widetilde I_1(K_1,\ldots,K_n)
 \ge n!c_n\prod_{i=1}^n|K_i|^{(n-1)/n}.$$

Haddad’s Lemma 5.1 and the equivalence of Equations (18)–(21) transfer this $p=1$ geometric inequality to the functional inequality for $\mathcal D_n$, with critical exponent $p^*=n/(n-1)=q$. At this endpoint his normalized weak profile for $K$ is $\mathbf1_K$, with the same surface-area measure as $K$ and $\left\lVert\mathbf 1_K\right\rVert_q=|K|^{(n-1)/n}$; see <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Haddad2020](#ref-Haddad2020), arXiv version, Equation (9) and Definition 2.2, pp. 8–9]. Consequently the functional and geometric sharp constants agree, as also follows from the constant relations after his Equation (21). This proves Equation <a href="#eq:mixed-affine-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mixed-affine-sobolev">[eq:mixed-affine-sobolev]</a>; taking all $f_i=f$ gives Equation <a href="#eq:affine-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-sobolev">[eq:affine-sobolev]</a>, the $p=1$ case of his Equation (22).

For sharpness, take nonnegative compactly supported smooth mollifications $f_j$ of the indicator of an ellipsoid $E$. They converge to $\mathbf1_E$ strictly in $BV(\mathbb R^n)$ and in $L^q$. Define finite measures $\nu_j$ on the sphere by $$\int\psi\,d\nu_j
 =\int_{\{\nabla f_j\ne0\}}
   \psi\!\left(-\frac{\nabla f_j}{|\nabla f_j|}\right)
   |\nabla f_j|\,dx,
 \qquad \psi\in C(S^{n-1}).$$ Reshetnyak’s continuity theorem <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Spector2011](#ref-Spector2011), author version, Theorem 1.3, p. 3] gives $\nu_j\rightharpoonup S_E$; the minus sign selects the outward normal in $D\mathbf1_E$. The product measures also converge weakly on the compact product sphere. Since the absolute determinant is continuous and homogeneous in each variable, this gives $\mathcal D_n(f_j,\ldots,f_j)\to
\widetilde I_1(E,\ldots,E)=n!|\Pi E|$. Since $\left\lVert\mathbf 1_E\right\rVert_q^n=|E|^{n-1}$, ellipsoid equality in Petty’s inequality gives the stated constants in the limit. This is the weak $p=1$ interpretation of sharpness. ◻

</details>

To compare the diagonal statement with Zhang’s affine Sobolev inequality, let $f\ne0$ be as in Corollary <a href="#cor:affine-sobolev" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:affine-sobolev">21</a> and define the origin-symmetric convex body $Z_f$ by $$h_{Z_f}(u)=\frac12\int_{\mathbb R^n}|u\cdot\nabla f(x)|\,dx.$$ This integral is positive for every unit vector $u$: otherwise $f$ would be constant on every line parallel to $u$, contradicting compact support and $f\ne0$. Thus $Z_f$ has nonempty interior. Haddad’s zonoid identity gives $|Z_f|=\mathcal D_n(f,\ldots,f)/n!$, and his Blaschke–Santaló comparison <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Haddad2020](#ref-Haddad2020), arXiv version, Equations (24)–(25), p. 17] yields $$|Z_f^\circ|^{-1/n}
 \ge \left(\frac{\mathcal D_n(f,\ldots,f)}{n!\kappa_n^2}\right)^{1/n}
 \ge \frac{\kappa_{n-1}}{\kappa_n}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_q.$$ The resulting bound on $|Z_f^\circ|$ is Zhang’s sharp affine Sobolev inequality <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Zhang1999](#ref-Zhang1999)]. Thus Equation <a href="#eq:affine-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-sobolev">[eq:affine-sobolev]</a> is a stronger affine $L^1$ inequality; the case $f=0$ is immediate. This application uses only $p=1$ and gives no conclusion for Haddad’s $p>1$ problems.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Holmes–Thompson isoperimetry

<a id="stmt-cor-holmes-thompson"></a>
<div id="cor:holmes-thompson" class="corollary">

**Corollary 22** (Holmes–Thompson isoperimetry). *Let $n\ge3$, let $B=-B\subset\mathbb R^n$ be the unit ball of a real normed space, and let $C\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex body. Write $B^\circ$ for the polar unit ball and put $I_B=\Pi(B^\circ)/\kappa_{n-1}$. Normalize the Holmes–Thompson volume and boundary area by $$\mathcal V_B(C)=\frac{|B^\circ|}{\kappa_n}|C|,
 \qquad
 \mathcal A_B(\partial C)=nV(C[n-1],I_B),$$ where $V(C[n-1],I_B)$ is mixed volume with $n-1$ copies of $C$, normalized by $V(C[n])=|C|$. Then <a id="eq:holmes-thompson"></a>$$\label{eq:holmes-thompson}
 \mathcal A_B(\partial C)^n
 \ge n^n\kappa_n\,\mathcal V_B(C)^{n-1}.$$ Equality holds if and only if $B$ is an ellipsoid and $C=x+tB$ for some $x\in\mathbb R^n$ and $t>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-holmes-thompson"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-holmes-thompson</summary>

*Proof.* The fixed-norm isoperimetrix formula and the equivalence with symmetric unpolarized Petty are classical; see Martini–Mustafaev <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[MartiniMustafaev2008](#ref-MartiniMustafaev2008), Definition 1(i), Equation (2), Section 5, and Theorem 12]. Minkowski’s mixed-volume inequality gives $$\frac{\mathcal A_B(\partial C)^n}{\mathcal V_B(C)^{n-1}}
 \ge n^n\frac{\kappa_n^{n-1}}{\kappa_{n-1}^{n}}R_n(B^\circ)
 \ge n^n\kappa_n.$$ The last step applies Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> to $B^\circ$ for $n\ge4$, and the separate three-dimensional result <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026](#ref-ChenEtAl2026), Theorem 1.1] for $n=3$. Equality in the first step means that $C$ is homothetic to $I_B$, while equality in the last makes $B^\circ$, hence $B$, an ellipsoid. Then $I_B$ is homothetic to $B$, giving exactly the stated equality cases. ◻

</details>
